BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE

1. Executive Summary
Product Introduction
Anifrolumab (Saphnelo), a monoclonal antibody (mAb) that inhibits subunit 1 of the type I
interferon receptor (IFNAR1), is a new biological product. The proposed indication is treatment
(b) (4)
of adults with moderate to severe systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE)
(b) (4)
The proposed dosing regimen is 300 mg every 4 weeks administered as an intravenous
(IV) infusion.

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
A 351 (a) biologics license application (BLA) is submitted by AstraZeneca AB for IV
administration of anifrolumab for the treatment of adult patients with moderate to severe
(b) (4)
systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE),
SLE is a serious and potentially
life-threatening disease that can impact any organ system. This disease can cause significant
morbidity and mortality as well as impact patients’ function and quality of life. The toxicities
associated with both the approved drugs for SLE as well as off-label treatments commonly used
to treat SLE contribute to the long-term morbidity and mortality observed in patients with SLE.
Only one targeted therapy, belimumab, has been approved for SLE in the last 60 years.
To support the SLE indication, the Applicant provided results from two phase 3 studies,
D3461C00004 (04) and D3461C00005 (05) and one phase 2 study, CD-IA-MEDI-546-1013
(1013). These studies evaluated anifrolumab IV 300 mg as well as two additional IV doses (150
mg and 1000 mg) in a total of 1,123 adults with active SLE on stable immunosuppressive
background medications. From one phase 2 study (1013) and two phase 3 studies (04 & 05),
459 subjects were treated with the proposed dose of 300 mg IV compared to 468 subjects
treated with placebo. Patients with severe active lupus nephritis or severe active central
nervous system (CNS) lupus were excluded. In addition, cyclophosphamide and the use of other
biologic agents were not permitted during the studies.
In all three studies, the SLE Responder Index (SRI-4) was originally selected as the primary
endpoint, to establish efficacy. SRI-4 was included as the primary endpoint at week 24 in study
1013 and proposed as the primary endpoint at week 52 in study 05 and study 04. SRI-4 is a
composite clinical endpoint to assess clinical response in SLE and has been successfully used to
support the approval of belimumab. All three studies included the British Isles Lupus
Assessment Group (BILAG)-based Composite Lupus Assessment (BICLA) endpoint. The review
team notes that BICLA and SRI-4 are both considered clinically meaningful endpoints for
evaluating a treatment effect on clinical response in SLE. 1

1

Guidance for Industry: Systemic Lupus Erythematosus—Developing Medical Products for Treatment (June 2010);
https://www.fda.gov/media/71150/download

8
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE

In study 1013, a higher proportion of anifrolumab 300 mg IV subjects (34%) achieved the
primary endpoint, SRI-4 response with oral corticosteroid tapering at week 24, as compared to
the placebo group (18%) [odds ratio (90% CI): 2.4 (1.3, 4.3)]. Because study 1013 was designed
as a proof-of-concept and dose-ranging study and did not appropriately control for multiplicity
(see section 8.1), the primary endpoint of SRI-4 is viewed as only having achieved nominal
significance for benefit of anifrolumab 300 mg IV compared to placebo. However, study 1013
shared many of the features of the design and conduct of the phase 3 trials—it was
randomized, placebo-controlled, 52 weeks in duration, and assessed the proposed dosing
regimen in an appropriate patient population with respect to clinically relevant primary and
secondary efficacy endpoints—and estimated effects were consistent with meaningful benefit.
In addition to the results for the primary week 24 SRI-4 response endpoint, there was
supportive evidence for SRI-4 and BICLA endpoints at week 52 (without oral corticosteroid
tapering). At week 52, a higher proportion of anifrolumab 300 mg IV subjects (63%) achieved
SRI-4 response (without oral corticosteroid tapering) as compared to the placebo group (40%)
[risk difference (95% CI): 24.0 (10.9, 37.2)], and a higher proportion of anifrolumab 300 mg IV
subjects (54%) achieved the BICLA response (without oral corticosteroid tapering) at week 52 as
compared to the placebo group (26%) [risk difference (95% CI): 28.4 (15.3, 41.5)].
The first phase 3 study completed, study 05, did not show a statistically significant difference in
SRI-4 response rate between anifrolumab 300 mg IV and placebo groups at week 52
[anifrolumab 36% compared to placebo 40%; risk difference (95% CI): -4.2 (-14.2, 5.8)]. Upon
further review of the data, the Applicant indicated that some of the protocol-specified
restrictions on medication were not implemented as intended per protocol and were not
appropriate based on clinical practice. As a result, a number of subjects who would be
considered responders in clinical practice were deemed non-responders due to these overly
restrictive medication rules. These rules included restricted use of non-steroidal antiinflammatory drugs (NSAIDs) and limited oral corticosteroids (OCSs) used for mild SLE flares.
When restricted medication rules were revised in a post-hoc analysis of study 05, there was a
numerically higher SRI-4 response rate observed in the anifrolumab treatment groups when
compared to placebo, though not reaching nominal significance. While study 05 did not show
statistically significant improvement in the prespecified primary endpoint (SRI-4), it did show
nominally significant changes in the key secondary BICLA endpoint in favor of anifrolumab 300
mg IV dose. Specifically, under the revised restricted medication rules, at week 52, a higher
proportion of anifrolumab 300 mg IV subjects (47%) achieved BICLA response as compared to
the placebo group (30%) [risk difference (95% CI): 17.0 (7.2, 26.8)]. Also, disease activity
measured by the SLE disease activity index (updated in 2000), (SLEDAI-2K), showed a dosedependent separation in favor of anifrolumab in study 05.
Noting the treatment effects in BICLA response rate in studies 05 and 1013, the Applicant
proposed changing the primary endpoint to BICLA response rate at week 52 for the second
phase 3 study (study 04) prior to database lock and unblinding. In May 2019, with agreement
from the Division, the Applicant amended the protocol and statistical analysis plan for the
9
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
ongoing second phase 3 trial, study 04, to change the primary endpoint from SRI-4 to BICLA
response as well as to modify the restricted medication rules.
Study 04 showed favorable, clinically meaningful, and statistically significant changes on the
primary (BICLA) and secondary endpoints (including SRI-4) when comparing anifrolumab 300
mg IV versus placebo. Specifically, at week 52, a higher proportion of anifrolumab 300 mg IV
subjects (48%) achieved BICLA response as compared to the placebo group (31%) [risk
difference (95% CI): 16.3 (6.4, 26.3)]. Further, at week 52, a higher proportion of anifrolumab
300 mg IV subjects (56%) achieved SRI-4 response as compared to the placebo group (37%) [risk
difference (95% CI): 18.2 (8.10, 28.3)]. The results for BICLA and SRI-4 in study 04 were
consistent regardless of which medication restriction rules were applied, i.e., original versus
amended.
A drug’s effectiveness must be established by substantial evidence 2. While this has often been
interpreted as requiring two adequate and well-controlled trials, there are other circumstances
where substantial evidence of effectiveness can be provided outside of this setting. For
example, there are situations when FDA finds one adequate and well-controlled clinical
investigation with confirmatory evidence, including supportive data outside of a controlled trial,
is sufficient to establish effectiveness 3. In particular, when the disease is life-threatening or
severely debilitating with an unmet need, additional flexibility may be warranted. The team
notes that a significant unmet medical need exists for safe and efficacious treatment for
patients with SLE, which was a consideration in the assessment of effectiveness in the
anifrolumab application.
In this case, 04 represents an adequate and well-controlled trial showing favorable and
statistically significant changes on the pre-specified, clinically meaningful primary and
secondary endpoints when comparing anifrolumab 300 mg IV versus placebo. Because the
phase 2 trial (1013) was designed only as a proof of concept and dose-ranging study with
inadequate multiplicity control and study 05, the first phase 3 trial, failed on its primary
endpoint, these cannot be considered additional successful adequate and well-controlled trials
when considered independently. However, the review team feels that when these trials are
considered in combination, the consistent results observed in studies 1013 and 05 do provide
confirmatory evidence that support the effectiveness of anifrolumab 300 mg IV.
Across the three studies, there are three randomized comparisons, each with adequate
duration (52 weeks), that support the claim that anifrolumab 300 mg IV yields higher BICLA
response rates compared to placebo. Further, two of the three studies provide support that
anifrolumab 300 mg IV yields higher SRI-4 response rates compared to placebo at week 52.
While study 05 failed to show an effect on SRI-4, the study showed a numerically dosedependent trend that is not statistically significant in SLEDAI-2K, a measure of disease activity

2

The FD&C Act section 505(d) (21 U.S.C. § 355(d))
https://www.fda.gov/media/133660/download

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
that is used to define both SRI-4 and BICLA and for which there was relatively consistent
evidence across all three studies. In all three studies, additional supportive evidence was noted
in the analyses of the secondary endpoints, including a greater proportion of subjects on
anifrolumab than placebo able to reduce oral corticosteroid use and a lower proportion of
subjects on anifrolumab experiencing a flare of SLE disease activity, further supporting the
effectiveness of anifrolumab 300 mg IV.
The current understanding of the role of Type I IFN in the pathogenesis of SLE 4,5 supports the
biological plausibility of targeting Type I IFN with anifrolumab in SLE and provides additional
confirmatory evidence of the effectiveness of anifrolumab. In addition to the evidence provided
from the clinical trials, mechanistic evidence is provided by the observed neutralization of the
type I IFN gene signature in anifrolumab-treated subjects with a high baseline type I IFN gene
signature in all three studies.
Therefore, based on the data generated from study 04 and additional confirmatory evidence
from studies 1013 and 05, and mechanistic rationale, there is substantial evidence of
effectiveness of the 300 mg dosing regimen of anifrolumab IV as treatment for adult patients
with active SLE who are receiving standard SLE therapy consisting of either one or any
combination of oral corticosteroids (OCS), antimalarials and/or immunosuppressants at
baseline.
The safety of anifrolumab was evaluated in a total of 837 adult SLE patients who received IV
anifrolumab (150 mg, 300 mg, or 1000 mg) during clinical trials. Of these, 688 SLE patients were
exposed to anifrolumab for at least 1 year and 263 patients were exposed for at least 3 years.
Integrated safety evaluation is based on two placebo-controlled datasets—one assessed
through 52 weeks and one based on the 52-week controlled period and a placebo-controlled
long-term extension up to three additional years. The three trials described above (1013, 05
and 04) provide data for 459 subjects treated with anifrolumab 300 compared with 466 on
placebo for 52 weeks. Patients enrolled in the two phase 3 trials (05 and 04) who received
placebo every 4 weeks for 52 weeks were randomized in a blinded fashion to continue on
placebo or initiate treatment with anifrolumab 300 mg. Long-term comparisons including
exposure beyond 52 weeks were measured for patients who were initially randomized to
anifrolumab and continued on anifrolumab beyond 52 weeks, compared to patients who were
initially randomized to placebo and later randomized to placebo beyond 52 weeks; in this
dataset, there was greater overall exposure to anifrolumab than placebo as more patients from
the anifrolumab arms than placebo arms continued beyond 52 weeks of treatment within the
long-term extension study.

Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE

Overall, the safety profile of anifrolumab IV 300 mg IV every 4 weeks in the adult SLE
population that participated in studies 1013, 04, and 05 (52-week controlled dataset) appeared
to be similar to placebo, with the exception of certain infections (including herpes zoster) and
infusion related reactions (including two cases of angioedema). One case of anaphylaxis was
reported in a subject receiving the 150 mg dose. In addition, beyond 52 weeks (phase 3 longterm extension dataset), herpes zoster and other viral infections continued to occur at a higher
rate in patients treated with anifrolumab 300 mg, and fatal respiratory infections occurred only
in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm, including pneumonia and COVID-19 versus placebo.
In summary, the efficacy and safety data generated from studies 1013, 04, and 05, support a
favorable benefit-risk assessment for approval of the 300 mg dosing regimen of anifrolumab IV
as add-on treatment for adult patients with active SLE while receiving standard SLE therapy
consisting of either one or any combination of oral corticosteroids (OCS), antimalarials and/or
immunosuppressants at baseline.

Benefit-Risk Assessment
Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
To support the proposed indication, the Applicant provided the results from three clinical studies—phase 2 study CD-IA-MEDI-546-1013 (1013)
and phase 3 studies D3461C00004 (04) and D3461C00005 (05), that evaluated anifrolumab IV 300 mg as well as two additional doses (150 mg
and 1000 mg) in a total of 1,123 adults with active systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) on stable immunosuppressive background medications.
These were international, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, 52-week studies that evaluated the efficacy, safety, and
immunogenicity of the proposed dose of 300 mg IV every 4 weeks in 925 adult subjects with active SLE receiving standard therapy.
Determination of substantial evidence of effectiveness in adult patients was based on efficacy results from one adequate and well-controlled
study—phase 3 study (04). This was an appropriately designed, well-conducted study that adequately controlled for multiplicity, to characterize
the efficacy and safety of anifrolumab in an adult population with active SLE. In study 04, anifrolumab 300 mg achieved a statistically significant
and clinically meaningful higher response rate compared to placebo in the primary endpoint of BICLA at week 52, a composite of multiple
measures of SLE improvement—anifrolumab 48% compared to placebo 31%, [risk difference (95% CI): 16.3 (6.3, 26.3)]). Additional supportive
evidence for efficacy is provided in this study by the SRI-4 composite 52-week endpoint, where a higher proportion of anifrolumab 300mg IV
subjects (56%) achieved response compared to the placebo group (37%)[risk difference (95% CI): 18.2 (8.1, 28.3)].
Confirmatory evidence of effectiveness is provided by study 1013, a phase 2 study designed to provide proof of concept and dose-ranging
information, and a second phase 3 study (study 05). While an appropriate method for controlling multiplicity was not pre-specified in study
1013, a higher proportion of anifrolumab 300 mg IV subjects (53.5%) achieved the 52-week BICLA secondary endpoint as compared to the
placebo group (25.7%)[risk difference (95% CI): 28.4 (15.3, 41.5)]. Additional supportive evidence for efficacy is provided by the SRI-4 composite
52-week endpoint in study 1013, for which a higher proportion of anifrolumab 300 mg IV subjects (62.6%) achieved response as compared to
the placebo group (40.2%)[risk difference (95% CI):24.0 (10.9, 37.2)]. While a second phase 3 study (study 05) did not meet its primary
objective and failed on the primary endpoint SRI-4, the results on BICLA and other secondary endpoints are largely consistent with effects
observed in study 04 and study 1013 and are viewed as additional confirmatory evidence. In study 05, a higher proportion of anifrolumab 300
mg IV subjects achieved the 52-week BICLA endpoint over placebo. This effect was consistently observed in a post-hoc analysis (using revised
restricted medication rules that are more consistent with clinical practice)—a higher proportion of anifrolumab 300 mg IV subjects (47%)
achieved the 52-week BICLA endpoint as compared to the placebo group (30%)[risk difference (95% CI): 17.0 (7.2, 26.8)].
The proposed dose of 300 mg IV demonstrated consistent benefit both for the BICLA endpoint as well as for secondary endpoints of reduction
13
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
in daily steroid dose and reduction in SLE flare rate.
The safety of anifrolumab 300 mg IV every 4 weeks in the SLE clinical program was consistent with expected increased risk of infection due to
immunosuppression, in particular for viral infections in which Type I IFN is a central part of the immune response. While the 52-week incidence
of serious infections between placebo and anifrolumab 300 mg IV arms in all three studies submitted to support approval was balanced
between anifrolumab and placebo arms—anifrolumab 4.8% (22/459) vs placebo 5.6% (26/466)—the proportion of subjects with any infections
was higher in subjects treated with anifrolumab 300 mg IV (69.7%; 320/459) as compared to subjects in the placebo arms (55.4%; EAIR
99.9/100 PY). Herpes zoster infections, in particular, occurred at a higher rate in anifrolumab-treated subjects compared to placebo (6.1% vs
1.3%). In the 52-week controlled study period, fatal infections occurred in 0.4% of patients receiving anifrolumab and 0.2% of patients receiving
placebo. In long-term, placebo controlled assessment of subjects on anifrolumab 300 mg beyond 52 weeks, a total of seven deaths were
attributed to infections in anifrolumab-treated subjects (four pneumonia and three COVID-19) as compared to none on placebo. Overall, more
cases of hypersensitivity reactions and infusion-related reactions (including two cases of angioedema) occurred in subjects receiving
anifrolumab compared to placebo, and one case of anaphylaxis occurred in a patient receiving the 150 mg dose.
In summary, the efficacy and safety data generated from one adequate and well-controlled study—04—as well as confirmatory evidence from
studies 1013 and 05, result in a favorable benefit-risk assessment for approval of the 300 mg IV every 4 weeks dosing regimen of anifrolumab
as add-on treatment for adult patients with active SLE while receiving standard therapy consisting of either one or any combination of oral
corticosteroids (OCS), antimalarials and/or immunosuppressants.
A significant unmet medical need exists for safe and efficacious treatment for patients with SLE. In addition to the evidence provided from the
clinical trials, the current understanding of the role of Type I IFN in the pathogenesis of SLE supports the biological plausibility of targeting Type
I IFN with anifrolumab in SLE .
If approved, anifrolumab, a first-in-class biologic with a unique mechanism of action, will provide a new treatment option for patients with SLE.

2. Therapeutic Context
Analysis of Condition
Systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) is a chronic, multisystem, disabling autoimmune rheumatic
disease of unknown etiology. Clinical manifestations of SLE can include constitutional
symptoms, alopecia and rashes, serositis, inflammatory arthritis, renal disease, systemic
vasculitis, lymphadenopathy, splenomegaly, hemolytic anemia, cognitive dysfunction, and
other central nervous system involvement. SLE affects approximately 200,000 to 1.5 million
people in the United States and at least five million people worldwide, 1415 with a particularly
higher prevalence among racial/ethnic minority groups. 16 SLE is a serious disease associated
with increased morbidity and mortality with an estimate that between 10-15% of people with
lupus will die prematurely due to complications of lupus. 17 Uncontrolled, ongoing disease
activity over time has been associated with poorer outcomes, such as organ damage and
coronary artery disease in SLE. 18 Higher doses of medications to control disease activity can also
be harmful for many patients as they are associated with cumulative and irreversible organ
damage (such as premature cataracts, retinopathy, osteoporotic fractures, cardiovascular
damage, avascular necrosis, and infections). 19
Available Therapies
Available therapies to treat SLE are summarized in Table 1. Prior to the recent approval of
belimumab, only three other drugs were specifically approved for SLE—aspirin and oral
corticosteroids (OCSs) (both circa 1950) and hydroxychloroquine in 1955. For mild disease, first
line treatments include anti-malarials (hydroxychloroquine) and OCSs (e.g., prednisone). Longterm use of hydroxychloroquine can cause retinopathy and poor adherence to treatment
remains an issue with this drug. 20 Steroids remain a mainstay of treatment for mild to severe
disease, however chronic steroid use is a contributing factor in long-term morbidity and early

cardiovascular mortality in patients with SLE. 21 Additional treatment options for moderate to
severe disease include immunosuppressants, such as methotrexate, azathioprine, and
mycophenolate mofetil. The use of immunosuppressants is associated with an increased risk of
infection, malignancy, cardiovascular disease, and bone marrow suppression, and are not
effective in all patients for all manifestations of SLE. 22 Only one targeted therapy has been
approved for SLE in the last 60 years. Belimumab is a neutralizing anti-B-lymphocyte stimulator
mAb. 23 It targets one pathway, while patients are likely to have different underlying
immunopathological pathways driving their SLE disease manifestations. 24 As such, it is not
effective in all patients. There is substantial unmet medical need in the treatment of SLE,
particularly in patients with moderate or severe disease.

3. Regulatory Background
U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
Anifrolumab is a new molecular entity that is not currently marketed in the US or any other country.
The current application is the initial BLA for anifrolumab and has been submitted to the Division of
Rheumatology and Transplant Medicine for the proposed indication for the treatment of adult
(b) (4)
patients with moderate to severe systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE),
.

Summary of Presubmission/Submission Regulatory Activity
The Agency had multiple interactions with the Applicant regarding the anifrolumab IV clinical
program. At an end-of-phase 2 meeting on September 16, 2014, the Agency provided feedback
on dose selection, endpoints, primary analyses, and controlling type 1 error in the phase 3
study designs for the clinical program. On May 24, 2018, the Agency provided written
responses to a type C meeting request on the overall content, format, and proposed data
presentation for the planned Biologics License Application (BLA). The FDA provided additional
feedback on the Integrated Summary of Efficacy (ISE), Integrated Summary of Safety (ISS), and
the Integrated Summary of Immunogenicity (ISE) for this BLA on October 29, 2018. For the BLA,
the Applicant proposed to provide data from a phase 2 study (1013), and two phase 3 studies
(04, 05).
Protocol Amendment 1—Change in Primary Endpoint
An amended protocol and statistical analysis plan for study 04 was submitted on May 31, 2019,
after both phase 3 studies were completed; however, only the data for study 05 had been
unblinded and analyzed. The database for study 05 was locked for the 52-week treatment
period in August 2018. The Applicant reported that the study did not meet the primary
endpoint of a statistically significant reduction in disease activity, as measured by SLE
Responder Index of at least 4 (SRI-4) at 52 weeks. However, the Applicant indicated that
secondary endpoints that were not SRI-4-based suggested there was a potentially clinically
meaningful response to anifrolumab. Therefore, the Applicant proposed to change the primary
and some key secondary endpoints for study 04 prior to its database lock and unblinding. The
timeline for key activities leading to changes in the primary endpoint and key secondary
endpoints in study 04 are summarized in Table 2.

The proposed changes to the primary and key secondary endpoints for study 04 were:
x Change from initial primary endpoint of SRI-4 response at week 52 to BICLA response at
week 52
x Change in key secondary endpoint from SRI-4 response at week 52 in IFN test high
subgroup to BICLA response at week 52 in IFN test high subgroup
x Added key secondary endpoint of proportion of subjects with 50% reduction in number
of swollen and tender joints at week 52 in subjects with 6 swollen and tender joints at
baseline
x SRI-4 response at week 24 demoted from key to other secondary endpoint
x No change in key secondary endpoints of proportion of subjects who achieve an OCS
dose 7.5 gm/day at week 40 maintained through week 52; proportion of subjects with
50% reduction in CLASI activity score at week 12 in subgroup with baseline CLASI 10;
annualized flare rate defined by 1 or more new BILAG-2004 A or 2 or more new B items
compared to previous visit
The Applicant stated that the BICLA endpoint may have some advantages over the SRI-4
because (1) the 97 questions of the BILAG-2004 may assess more of the clinical diversity of the
disease and (2) the BILAG-2004 requires the clinician to rate manifestations with multiple
categories, rather than simply absent or present. The Applicant argues that this endpoint may
be more comprehensive, give more balanced weight to affected body systems, and measure
incremental improvements or worsening. The Applicant also notes that the BICLA endpoint
retains the SLEDAI-2K, using it (alongside PGA) to exclude patients who worsen in ways not
captured by BILAG-2004 from being classified as responders. Therefore, the Applicant proposed
to replace the SRI-based endpoints in the hierarchy for study 04 with BICLA-based endpoints.
Protocol Amendment 2—Change in Restricted Medication Rules

26
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
In the phase 2 study and both phase 3 studies, patients randomized to treatment continued to
receive standard of care therapy with OCSs, antimalarial agents, and/or immunosuppressants in
addition to being randomized to receive either anifrolumab or placebo. The primary and several
of the secondary endpoints, including all key secondary endpoints, were composite endpoints
that include the condition “no use of restricted medications beyond the protocol-allowed
threshold before assessment” in order for subjects to be considered responders. However, the
Applicant indicated that, upon review of the unblinded data from study 05 after the week 52
database lock, some of the protocol-specified restrictions on medication were “not
implemented as intended per protocol and were not appropriate based on clinical practice,” in
particular regarding use of NSAIDs. A revised set of restricted medication rules, whereby every
class of medication was reviewed to ensure clinical appropriateness, were applied to study data
as post-hoc analyses in study 05 and were proposed to evaluate the unblinded phase 3 study
(study 04). The change in restricted medication rules for NSAIDs, which had the largest impact
on determination of responders versus non-responders (NR; due to taking a restricted
medication), was as follows:
“Patients were classified as NR for all subsequent efficacy assessments if a new NSAID
was initiated and/or there was an increase in NSAID dosage after day 1.”
was changed to:
“Initiation of a new NSAID within 14 days of week 52 and documented use on the day
before the week-52 assessment led to classification as NR at the week-52 visit. NSAID
use prior to week 50 did not lead to classification as NR.”
The May 2019 submission included the amended clinical study protocol and statistical analysis
plan which reflect changes to the primary and key secondary endpoints as well as modifications
to the restricted medication rules. The Division cautioned that the revision in the statistical
plan to incorporate changes to the medication rules and primary endpoint may affect the
overall interpretability of efficacy in the overall anifrolumab IV clinical program for SLE.
At a pre-BLA Type B meeting with the Applicant on February 20, 2020, the following
comments/recommendations were communicated to the Applicant to facilitate review of the
pending BLA submission:
1) Provide a justification for the discrepancies in the different study results observed.
Specifically, the study design and patient populations appear to be similar between
Studies 04 and 05; however, the differences in the results from these two studies are
difficult to reconcile.
2) For each study (Study 04, 05, and 1013), present the results for BICLA and SRI-4
response rates using (a) the original medication rules, (b) the updated medication rules,
and (c) ignoring adherence to medication rules or discontinuation treatment in
determining response (i.e., using all observed data regardless of adherence).
3) Provide an examination of the components of the SRI-4 and BICLA endpoints for each
27
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
study (Study 04, 05, and 1013). It will be important to evaluate the treatment effect on
the overall endpoint and each of the components of the composite outcome in
supportive analyses based on all observed data collected, regardless of treatment
discontinuation or protocol adherence. This will be particularly important given the
uncertainty surrounding the medication rules for these studies.
4) Include a tipping point analysis that targets the treatment policy estimand, i.e., includes
the observed data for subjects who would be considered non-responders due to
treatment discontinuation or lack of adherence to restricted medication rules and
consider.
5) Include adequate documentation and programs to facilitate replication for all analyses,
including any post-hoc analyses, supplementary analysis, and sensitivity analyses
presented.

Medical Policy and Program Review Council (MPPRC) Meeting January 22, 2021
The Division sought input from the MPPRC on the approvability of anifrolumab for treatment of
moderate to severe SLE as well as any additional recommendations.
MPPRC Summary Recommendations: Given the markedly positive study 04, consistency with
study 1013, and supportive evidence from the post hoc analysis of study 05, the Council deemed
the evidence for effectiveness persuasive. In general, the Council considered two approaches,
first considering 1013 an AWC (adequate and well-controlled) study, if sufficiently justified from
statistical perspective, and study 04 as the second AWC study providing substantial evidence.
Further discussions regarding the multiplicity control in 1013 were recommended. The second
approach would be to consider 04 as the single AWC trial, and the other clinical data as
providing confirmatory evidence.

Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
Clinical data from three studies (D3461C0004, D3461C00005, and CD-IA-MEDI-546-1013) were
submitted to the Agency in support of a biologics licensing application (BLA) 761123 for
anifrolumab to treat adult patients with moderate to severe, auto-antibody positive systemic
(b) (4)
lupus erythematosus (SLE)
Clinical inspections of the Applicant
AstraZeneca Pharmaceuticals L.P., and four clinical investigators (Dr. Anurekha Chadha, site
7904, Dr. Sabeen Najam, site 7834, Dr. Phillip Waller, site 7815, and Dr. Eric Lee, site 7825)
were conducted in support of this application.
Maintenance of blinding was adequate throughout for all three studies (DC346C00004,
DC346100005, CD-IA-MEDI-546-1013.) The data and safety monitoring board (DSMB)
consisted of multi-disciplinary group of two rheumatologists, one infectious disease, one
nephrologist, and one biostatistician. On 1/25/2019, DSMB minutes document how study
D3461C00005 did not meet the primary efficacy endpoint. Study D3461C00005 was closed out
(b) (4)
and high level, aggregate efficacy and safety data were presented to the full DSMB,
and
(b) (4)
(b) (4)
attendees. The Applicant and
medical team remained blinded to study D3461C00004.
The DSMB reviewed the Closed Report (partially unblinded by masked treatment arms) for
study D3461C00004 during the closed session.
In general, the Applicant appeared to be in compliance with Good Clinical Practices. A Form
FDA 483 (Inspectional Observations) was not issued. Data submitted by this Applicant appear
acceptable in support of this biologic license application.
Based on the results of the clinical investigators and Applicant inspections, Protocols
D3461C0004, D3461C00005, and CD-IA-MEDI-546-1013 appear to have been conducted
adequately, and the data generated appear acceptable in support of the proposed indication.
Of note, the OSI review team identified issues with the data generated from two sites, Dr.
Sabeen Najam and Dr. Phillip Waller, because of failure to prepare and maintain adequate and
accurate source records for study D3461C00004. However, this was limited to the two sites and
was considered related to the use of site-specific software, which was used only by these two
sites. No data integrity issues were identified at Anurekha Chadha and Dr. Eric Lee sites. A
sensitivity analysis conducted by our statistical reviewers confirms that the exclusion of the
data generated from Dr. Sabeen Najam and Dr. Phillip Waller sites does not impact the overall
efficacy conclusions.
Overall, based on completed OSI inspections as well as robustness of the efficacy data, the
conclusions on efficacy to support approval are reliable.

Product Quality
The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality (OPQ), CDER, recommends approval of STN 761123 for
anifrolumab manufactured by AstraZeneca. The data submitted in this application are adequate
to support the conclusion that the manufacture of anifrolumab is well-controlled and leads to a
product that is pure and potent. It is recommended that this product be approved for human
use under conditions specified in the package insert.
(b) (4)

Glycosylation analysis of anifrolumab showed that it contains
% galactose-alpha-1,3galactose (alpha-gal) on the product Fc domain. Alpha-gal on proteins is a known allergen that
may produce anti-alpha-gal IgE mediated anaphylactic and hypersensitivity reaction. Detail
analysis of the clinical data, especially the anaphylaxis and hypersensitivity cases, demonstrated
that risk of alpha-gal associated hypersensitivity and anaphylaxis for SAPHNELO was low (see
clinical section for detail).

5. Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
Executive Summary
The Applicant has conducted a comprehensive program of pharmacology, pharmacokinetic
(PK), and toxicology studies with anifrolumab to support clinical development and the BLA
submission. All studies submitted to support approval have been reviewed previously under
IND 101849. The key findings from the nonclinical studies are cited and summarized in this
review to support the approval of BLA 761123. The Applicant has also submitted extractables
and leachables studies and safety assessments, which have been reviewed under this BLA (see
Section 16.3). The safety assessment of impurities from the drug substance and drug product is
reviewed in Section 16.3.
Pharmacology studies demonstrated that anifrolumab bound to IFNAR1 and inhibited the
expression of Type-I IFN responsive genes in peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) and
the expression of differentiation markers and cytokines/chemokines from monocytes. An
anifrolumab mouse surrogate was effective in a murine model of scleroderma and an
accelerated NZB/W F1 murine model of lupus.
Anifrolumab had no or reduced binding to Fc Gamma Receptors I (Fc RI), Fc RIIA, Fc RIIB, or
Fc RIIIA in vitro. No complement dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) or antibody dependent cellular
cytotoxicity (ADCC) were noted in vitro. In vitro anifrolumab-positive human and cynomolgus
monkey tissue binding was consistent with published reports of broad expression of IFNAR in
the corresponding tissues.
The effect of anifrolumab on the cardiovascular (CV) system was evaluated in a GLP-compliant
39-week general IV toxicity study in monkeys (Study# 7140-129). In this study, cynomolgus
monkeys were given 40 IV infusion (30 minutes) doses of anifrolumab at 5 and 50 mg/kg/week
once a week (QW) or 40 subcutaneous (SC) injection doses of anifrolumab at 15 and 60
mg/kg/week QW. Electrocardiography (ECG) parameters and blood pressure (BP) parameters
were measured once during the predose phase, on Days 3 and 276 of the dosing phase, and on
Day 83 of the recovery phase for these animals after they were anesthetized with ketamine. No
drug-related adverse CV findings were noted. In another 4-week IV toxicity study in cynomolgus
monkeys with lower systemic exposures (see Section 5.4), ECG parameters and BP were
(b) (4)
measured within 1 hr. post dosing on Day 22 (weekly dose, Study#
263.01). No drugrelated adverse CV findings were noted.
GLP-compliant toxicology studies up to 39 weeks indicated that anifrolumab was well-tolerated
in cynomolgus monkeys. No drug-related adverse clinical sign changes were noted. No drugrelated CV toxicity was noted. Besides anti-drug antibody (ADA)-induced minimal arteritis in
multiple organs in a few male animals given 40 doses of anifrolumab at 50 mg/kg/week (IV) or
60 mg/kg/week (SC), no additional drug-related adverse effect was noted. Further, anifrolumab
31
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
was not found to induce reproductive and developmental toxicity. In the GLP-compliant 39week toxicity study in sexually mature cynomolgus monkeys, no drug-related effects on
reproductive organs/tissues, semen analysis, spermatogenesis analysis, or menstrual cycle were
present. In the GLP-compliant enhanced prenatal and postnatal development (ePPND) study in
pregnant cynomolgus monkeys, no drug-related adverse effects in maternal animals or infants
(body growth, behavior, functional, morphological, and skeletal development, and T-celldependent antibody response) were noted. The plasma exposures of anifrolumab at the noobserved-adverse-effect levels (NOAELs) for general toxicity and reproductive and
developmental toxicity are >59- and 28-fold higher than the human exposure (AUC) at the
MRHD.
Genetic toxicity studies have not been conducted with anifrolumab as they are generally not
applicable for biotechnology-derived pharmaceuticals. Carcinogenicity studies have not been
conducted with anifrolumab, however the existing literature from studies with IFNAR1- and
IFNAR2-deficient mice and mice administered IFN / and anti-IFN- / antibodies suggested
that IFNAR blockade increased carcinogenic potential in rodents. The Applicant and review
team have agreed on proposed language to describe this finding based on the pharmacology of
anifrolumab in the product labeling.

Referenced NDAs, BLAs, DMFs
None

Pharmacology
Multiple in vitro pharmacology studies have been conducted identifying the mechanism of
action and activity of anifrolumab. None of the primary or secondary pharmacology studies
were conducted in compliance with GLP. In the opinion of the nonclinical reviewer, this is a
typical approach in drug research and does not affect the integrity of the application.
Primary pharmacology:
Anifrolumab is a first-in-class product targeting IFNAR1. An Established Pharmacologic Class
(EPC) assessment was conducted. Based on following studies, anifrolumab is considered a Type
I Interferon Receptor Antagonist.
x Anifrolumab has high affinity for IFNAR1 expressed on human PBMCs with KD of 0.29 nM
(Study# RIA546-0002);
x Anifrolumab bound to the sub-domain 3 of human IFNAR1 (265-307) with residues 276-279
being identified as the critical epitope (Study# RIA546-0004);
x Anifrolumab competitively inhibited the binding of IFN- -2a to IFNAR in Daudi cells (Study#
RIA546-0003);
x Anifrolumab inhibited
¾ IFN-stimulated response elements (ISRE) activation induced by all the recombinant
human IFN- subtypes (Study# RIA546-0006) and SLE sera (Study# RIA546-0008);

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE

x

¾ the expression of Type-I IFN responsive genes (USP18, HERC5/6, IFIT1/3/5, G1P2/3,
MX1/2, OAS1/2, IFI44, IFI44L, Ly6E) from PBMCs by sera of SLE patients (Study# RIA5460005);
¾ the expression of differentiation markers (CD123 and CD38) and cytokines/chemokines
(IL-6, IL-10, MCP-1, MCP-2) from monocytes by leukocyte-IFNs (Study# RIA546-0005);
Anti-murine IFNAR1 MAb 5A3 (anifrolumab surrogate) was effective in a murine model of

(Study# RIA546-0011).

In cross-species studies, anifrolumab was found to
x have high affinity for IFNAR1 expressed on cynomolgus monkey PBMCs with KD of 0.65 nM
(Study# RIA546-0002);
x inhibit ISRE activation induced by most cynomolgus recombinant type I IFNs with IC50 values
comparable to the corresponding human subtypes with the exception of the IFN- 1, IFN- 4,
and IFN- 6 subtypes (Study# RIA546-0006);
x have no effect on murine IFN- -mediated ISRE activation in cells expressing murine IFNAR1
(Study# RIA546-0006).
Secondary pharmacology:
x Anifrolumab had no or significantly reduced binding to Fc RI and Fc RIIIA in vitro (Study#
RIA546-0001);
x Anifrolumab had no binding to Fc RIIA or Fc RIIB in vitro (Study# RIA546-0010);
x Anifrolumab bound to Fc Gamma Neonatal Receptor (FcRn) with KD similar to wild type in
vitro, suggesting the normal IgG recycling property (Study# RIA546-0010);
x Anifrolumab did not induce CDC in human CD20+ IFNAR1+ target Daudi B cells (Study#
RIA546-0009);
x Anifrolumab did not induce ADCC in IFNAR expressing 293F cells (Study# RIA546-0001 and
RIA546-0012).

measured once during the predose phase, on Days 3 and 276 of the dosing phase, and on Day
83 of the recovery phase for these animals after they were anesthetized with ketamine. No
drug-related adverse CV findings were noted. In another 4-week IV toxicity study in cynomolgus
monkeys with lower systemic exposures (see Section 5.4), ECG parameters and BP were
(b) (4)
measured within 1 hr. post dosing on Day 22 (weekly dose, Study#
263.01). No drugrelated adverse CV findings were noted.

General toxicology; additional studies
In a GLP-compliant single-dose toxicity study, cynomolgus monkeys were given a single IV
infusion (30 minutes) dose of anifrolumab at 0.003, 0.03, 0.3, and 1 mg/kg (Study# 7140-131).
No drug-related toxicity was noted. ADA was detected in all dose groups with the highest
antibody titers occurring on Day 29 at 1 mg/kg. The NOAEL was 1 mg/kg (AUC0-t = 75.8
μg.d/mL).
In a GLP-compliant 4-week repeat-dose toxicity study with a 7-week recovery period,
cynomolgus monkeys were given 4 IV infusion (30 minutes) doses of anifrolumab at 0.5, 5, and
(b) (4)
30 mg/kg/week QW (Study#
263.01). The only toxicity noted was a transient (up to 4 days
during the first 9 days) finding of dry feces in a dose-related manner. No hypersensitivity
reaction was observed. ADA was detected in all dose groups, but the percentage of monkeys
with positive titers decreased with increasing anifrolumab doses. The NOAEL was 30 mg/kg
(AUC0-7d = 11,100 μg.d/mL).
Genetic Toxicology
Genetic toxicity studies have not been conducted with anifrolumab. Genetic toxicity studies are
generally not applicable for biotechnology-derived pharmaceuticals such as anifrolumab, as
discussed in the ICH S6(R1) guidance document.
Carcinogenicity
Carcinogenicity studies have not been conducted with anifrolumab. However, the existing
literature from studies with IFNAR1- and IFNAR2-deficient mice and mice administered IFN /
and anti-IFN- / antibodies suggested that IFNAR blockade increased carcinogenic potential in
rodents.

6. Clinical Pharmacology
Executive Summary
Anifrolumab is a monoclonal antibody (mAb) that inhibits subunit 1 of the type I interferon
receptor (IFNAR1). AstraZeneca submitted BLA 761123 on July 31, 2020 seeking the marketing
approval for anifrolumab for the treatment of adults with moderate to severe systemic lupus
(b) (4)
erythematosus (SLE)
The proposed dosing regimen is 300 mg every 4
weeks administered as an intravenous (IV) infusion.
BLA 761123 consists of eight clinical and clinical pharmacology studies, including two phase 1
studies, three phase 2 studies, and three phase 3 studies. In addition, three reports for
population PK analysis, exposure-response modeling, and integrated summary of
immunogenicity were submitted.
The following are the major clinical pharmacology findings of the current review:
1. Anifrolumab exhibits non-linear PK in the dose range of 100 mg to 1000 mg with AUC
increased more than dose-proportionally due to IFNAR1-mediated clearance. Following
the every 4 weeks IV administrations of anifrolumab, steady-state was reached by Day
85. The steady state exposure (AUC) for 1000 mg q4w is approximately 4 fold higher
compared to the steady state exposure of 300 mg q4w. The steady state exposure (AUC)
for 300 mg q4w is approximately 3 fold higher compared to the steady state exposure of
150 mg q4w.
2. The dosing regimen of anifrolumab has been adequately explored. Results from phase 2
dose-ranging study (1013) showed that while both 300 mg and 1000 mg q4w dosing
regimens were efficacious in SLE patients, 1000 mg dosing did not provide additional
treatment benefit. Therefore, IV 300 mg q4w dosing regimen was selected for phase 3
studies (04 and 05). A 150 mg dose group was included in the Phase 3 program to
further elucidate dose response. The responder rate was numerically lower at 150 mg
q4w. Exposure-response analysis for efficacy and safety indicated that the proposed IV
300 mg q4w dosing regimen provides the optimal benefit-risk balance in patients with
SLE.
3. No dose adjustment of anifrolumab is recommended for any intrinsic or extrinsic
factors.
4. In Phase 3 studies 04 and 05, 6 out of 352 subjects (1.7%) with anifrolumab IV 300 mg
q4w treatment were detected ADA positive post baseline. Due to the limited number of
subjects, no conclusion could be drawn regarding the impact of positive ADA on
anifrolumab PK, PD, efficacy, and safety.
5. In patients with SLE, following the administration of anifrolumab at 300 mg IV q4w for
52 weeks, neutralization ( 80%) of type I IFN gene signature was observed from week 4
to week 52 in blood samples of patients with elevated levels of type I IFN inducible
40
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
genes. This PD marker returned to baseline levels within 8 to 12 weeks following
withdrawal of anifrolumab at the end of the 52-week treatment period. However, the
clinical relevance of the type I IFN gene signature neutralization is unclear.
6. In SLE patients with positive anti-dsDNA antibodies at baseline (Studies 04 and 05),
treatment with anifrolumab 300 mg led to numerical reductions in anti-dsDNA
antibodies over time through week 52.
7. In patients with low complement levels (C3 and C4), increases in complement levels
were observed in patients receiving anifrolumab through week 52.

Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
The Office of Clinical Pharmacology, Division of Inflammation and Immune Pharmacology (DIIP)
and Division of Pharmacometrics (DPM) have reviewed the clinical pharmacology data
submitted under BLA 761123. This submission is recommended for approval from a clinical
pharmacology perspective for the treatment of patients with SLE.
Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
Anifrolumab is a mAb that inhibits IFNAR1. It exhibits non-linear PK in the dose range of 100 mg
to 1000 mg due to IFNAR1-mediated clearance. Population PK analysis showed baseline type I
IFN gene signature status and body weight have a significant impact on anifrolumab CL, but
were not considered clinically relevant.
General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
General Dosing
The proposed dosing regimen is 300 mg every 4 weeks administered as an IV infusion.
Therapeutic Individualization
None
Outstanding Issues
None

Comprehensive Clinical Pharmacology Review
General Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetic Characteristics
Anifrolumab is a mAb that inhibits IFNAR1. Based on population PK analysis, anifrolumab
exhibits non-linear PK in the dose range of 100 mg to 1000 mg with AUC increased more than

Reference ID: 4834322

Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE

anifrolumab is 0.193 L/day (Figure 1).
Following the every 4 weeks IV administrations of anifrolumab, steady-state was reached by
Day 85 (Figure 2). There was minimal accumulation in Cmax after multiple dosing with an
accumulation ratio of 1.36 at the 300 mg dose level and 1.43 at the 1000 mg dose level (Table
3). Mean Ctrough doubled after multiple doses with an accumulation ratio of 2.49 at the 300

pharmacokinetics, the steady state exposure (AUC) for 1000 mg q4w is approximately 4 fold
higher compared to the steady state exposure of 300 mg q4w. The steady state exposure (AUC)
for 300 mg q4w is approximately 3 fold higher compared to the steady state exposure of 150
mg q4w.

Clinical Pharmacology Questions
Does the clinical pharmacology program provide supportive evidence of effectiveness?
This submission consists of 8 clinical and clinical pharmacology studies, including 2 phase 1
studies, 3 phase 2 studies, and 3 phase 3 clinical studies (Table 4). The proposed indication of
anifrolumab was based on the three studies submitted to support approval (phase 2 study 1013
and phase 3 studies 04 and 05 (see section 7 and 8). The PD assessment of anifrolumab, i.e. the
21-gene type I IFN signature, provided supportive evidence of effectiveness.
What is the PD effect of Anifrolumab?
Type 1 IFN gene signature
Anifrolumab is a human IgG1 mAb that binds to IFNAR1 and inhibits type I IFN signaling by
blocking the biologic activity of type I IFNs. Type I IFN signaling leads to the transcription
activation and expression of over 100 genes, of which a subset of 21 genes was shown to be
robustly upregulated by type I IFNs and used to monitor the impact of IFNAR blockade in blood.
The 21-gene type I IFN signature was assessed in clinical studies as a PD biomarker of
anifrolumab activity.
The SLE patient population has been stratified based on a 4-gene type I IFN gene signature test,
where the genes in the subset were the most highly expressed in the target SLE patient
population and the test results (high or low) were based on the over-expression of type I IFNinducible genes. Approximately 75% patients in study 1013 and 82-83% patients in Studies 04
and 05 were type I IFN gene signature test high at baseline.
In the pooled PD analysis, 300 mg and 1000 mg resulted in 80% median neutralization of the
type I IFN gene signature by week 4 and attained over 52-week treatment period in patients
with high type I IFN gene signature, whereas a much less extent of PD neutralization was
observed in the 150 mg group (Figure 3). While neutralization of the type I IFN gene signature
reflects the direct pharmacological effect of anifrolumab and there is a clear dose-dependent
PD response, its clinical relevance is still unclear as the PD response is higher in the nonresponders on the 300 mg arm compared to the responders on the 150 mg arm (Figure 4).

Immunology/serology (anti-dsDNA antibodies, C3, C4)
In the pooled phase 3 studies (Studies 04 and 05), for SLE patients with positive anti-dsDNA
antibodies at baseline (median levels of anti-dsDNA were 50.1 U/mL and 53.0 U/mL, in the
anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo groups, respectively), treatment with anifrolumab 300 mg led
to numerical reductions in anti-dsDNA antibody levels over time through week 52 compared
with the placebo group (median change from baseline of -14.82 U/mL vs -5.37 U/mL at week
52).
47
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE

In the pooled phase 3 studies (Studies 04 and 05), the mean levels of C3 at baseline were 0.691
g/L in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and 0.701 g/L in the placebo group. For patients with an
abnormal C3 level at baseline, the anifrolumab 300 mg group had greater numerical increases
in C3 levels over the 52 weeks compared with the placebo group (mean change from baseline
of 0.13 g/L vs 0.04 g/L at week 52). The mean levels of C4 at baseline were 0.073 g/L in the
anifrolumab 300 mg group and 0.072 g/L in the placebo group. In patients with low/abnormal
complement levels at baseline, normalization of C4 was observed at week 52 in 22.6% (n=19) of
patients receiving anifrolumab and in 7.1% (n=6) of patients receiving placebo.
Is the proposed dosing regimen appropriate for the general patient population for which the
indication is being sought?
The proposed dosing regimen, IV 300 mg q4w, is appropriate for the general patient population
for the treatment of patients with SLE.
Phase 2 dose-ranging study
Study 1013 is a phase 2, multinational, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebocontrolled, parallel-group study to evaluate the efficacy and safety of 2 IV dosing regimens in
subjects with chronic, moderately-to-severely active SLE with an inadequate response to SOC
SLE. A total of approximately 300 subjects were randomized (1:1:1) to receive IV dose of
anifrolumab 300 or 1000 mg or placebo q4w for 48 weeks. Results indicated that both 300 mg
and 1000 mg treatment groups showed statistical improvement in the primary endpoint, SRI(4)
with OCS tapering at week 24, over placebo and the response rates were similar between 300
mg and 1000 mg (34.3% and 28.8%, respectively, versus 17.6% in placebo). Thus, 300 mg q4w
was considered appropriate for the Phase 3 program.
As 300 mg and 1000 mg appear to be on the plateau part of the dose-response curve for
efficacy, a 150 mg dose group was included in the Phase 3 program to elucidate dose response
and provide additional data to characterize benefit risk.

The Applicant conducted exposure response (ER) analysis based on the two phase 3 studies
with two dosing regimens (300 mg q4w, 150 mg q4w). The exposure response relationships for
efficacy endpoints (BICLA and SRI(4)) and the safety event of interest (herpes zoster) are
depicted in Figure 6 and Figure 7 below. For details in ER analysis, see section 16.4.3 Exposure
Response Relationship.

For the exposure-response for efficacy, for both BICLA and SRI(4), both responder rates were at
its maximal levels at 300 mg q4w without further improvement at 1000 mg q4w, and
numerically lower at 150 mg q4w. For the exposure-response for safety, the observed herpes
zoster incidence rates in the Phase 2 study were 2.0%, 5.1%, and 9.5% for placebo, 300 mg q4w,
and 1000 mg q4w, respectively. This result was consistent with the incidence rate observed in
the Phase 3 studies, and together supported a dose dependent herpes zoster incidence rate
from 150 mg to 1000 mg. These efficacy and safety ER analyses supported the 300 mg q4w
dosing regimen as the optimized dosing regimen.
Is an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy required for subpopulations based
on intrinsic patient factors?
No dose adjustment is needed for subpopulations based on intrinsic factors.
In population PK analysis, body weight and baseline type I IFN status were identified as
significant covariates. Patients with higher body weight and type I IFN test high patients were
found to have higher CL, however, the difference was not considered clinically relevant.
50
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
Patients with high UPCR appeared to have higher CL, but the impact on PK was not significant.
The exclusion criteria for UPCR was 2 mg/mg (or 226.30 mg/mmol) in the SLE clinical trials.
Other intrinsic factors such as race/ethnicity/region, age, sex, and renal/hepatic function tests
have no impact on the PK. See section 16.4 for details.
Are there clinically relevant food-drug or drug-drug interactions, and what is the appropriate
management strategy?
Anifrolumab is a mAb and proposed to be administered through IV infusion. There are no
clinically relevant food-drug or drug-drug interactions.
Based on population PK analysis, concomitant use of oral corticosteroids, anti-malarials,
immunosuppressants (azathioprine, methotrexate, mycophenolate mofetil, mycophenolic acid,
and mizorbine), NSAIDs, ACE inhibitors, and HMG-CoA reductase inhibitors did not significantly
affect the PK of anifrolumab.
What is the incidence of ADA? What is the impact of ADA on the PK, PD, safety, and clinical
outcomes of the biological product (e.g., therapeutic protein)?
In phase 3 studies 04 and 05, 6 out of 352 subjects (1.7%) with anifrolumab IV 300 mg q4w
treatment and 17 out of 357 subjects (4.8%) with placebo were detected ADA positive post
baseline. Due to the limited number of subjects, no conclusion could be drawn regarding the
impact of positive ADA on anifrolumab PK, PD, efficacy, and safety.
Refer to the OBP review regarding the bioanalytical assay for immunogenicity assessment.

Review Strategy
The clinical review program supporting registration of anifrolumab for the treatment of SLE
consisted of 1 Phase 2 trial (CD-IA-MEDI-546-1013) and 2 Phase 3 trials (D3461C00004 and
D3461C00005). See section 8.3 for a detailed description of these studies.
No pooling of data was performed for the efficacy analysis. For the evaluation of safety, the
52-week safety data for the 300 mg IV dose was summarized in the clinical study reports for
studies 1013, 04 and 05.
The application contained double-blind safety data from adult SLE patients collected over 52
weeks of 300 mg IV anifrolumab dosing in studies 1013, 04 and 05. Safety data for all 3 doses of
anifrolumab (150 mg, 300 mg, and 1000 mg) from studies 1013, 04 and 05 plus study 1145
(open-label extension from study 1013) and for the 300 mg dose in study 09 (placebocontrolled long-term extension from studies 04 and 05) were also included for completeness.

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE

8. Statistical and Clinical and Evaluation
Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
CD-IA-MEDI-546-1013 (Phase 2), D3461C00004 (Phase 3), and
D3461C00005 (Phase 3)
Trial Designs
Study 1013
Study CD-IA-MEDI-546-1013 (1013) was a 52-week, multi-center, randomized, double-blind,
placebo-controlled, parallel group study. Patients were randomized in a 1:1:1 fashion to MEDI546 (anifrolumab) 1000mg, MEDI-546 300mg, or placebo (Figure 8). Anifrolumab or placebo
was administered as an IV infusion via an infusion pump for approximately 60 minutes every 4
weeks for a total of 13 doses (first dose at Day 1 (week 0) and last dose at Day 337 (week 48)).
All patients were to be followed for 85 days after Day 337 through Day 422 (week 60).
Randomization was stratified by (SLEDAI-2K) score at screening (< 10 points vs 10 points), Day
1 OCS dose (< 10 mg/day vs 10 mg/day of prednisone or equivalent), and the results of a
diagnostic test for type I IFN signature in whole blood at screening using a 4-gene type I IFN test
(positive vs negative).
The study design included oral corticosteroid (OCS) tapering targets. Patients who experienced
an increase in SLE activity were permitted one burst of OCS or intramuscular corticosteroids
between Day 1 and 71, however, were required to taper to Day 1 levels or less within 14 days
and to complete by Day 71, otherwise they would be declared non-responders for the primary
and disease activity related secondary endpoints. Further, patients who required an increase of
OCS above Day 1 dose levels after Day 71 were to be declared non-responders for the primary
endpoint and disease activity related secondary endpoints at Day 169 (week 24). In addition,
patients who were unable to reduce OCS to < 10 mg/day and Day 1 dose of prednisone or
equivalent by Day 85 and unable to maintain OCS doses < 10 mg/day and Day 1 dose of
prednisone or equivalent until Day 169 were to be declared non-responders for the primary
endpoint at Day 169 (week 24).
Patients were also permitted to receive one additional burst of OCS or intramuscular
corticosteroids between Days 169 and 281 and were not to be declared a non-responder for
disease activity secondary endpoints at Day 365 (week 52) if they tapered to Day 169 levels or
less within 14 days and completed by Day 281. Patients who required an increase of OCS above
the minimum of Day 1 dose or Day 169 dose level after Day 281 were to be declared nonresponders for disease activity secondary endpoints at Day 365 (week 52). Patients who were
unable to reduce OCS to < 10 mg/day and Day 1 dose of prednisone or equivalent by Day 281
and unable to maintain OCS doses < 10 mg/day and Day 1 dose until Day 365 were to be
declared non-responders for certain disease activity secondary endpoints at Day 365 (week 52).
57
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
Investigators should have tapered OCS based on disease activity in all subjects throughout the
study, as permitted between Days 1 and 113 and between Days 169 and 309. Patients who
permanently discontinued from receiving investigational product were to be followed for
safety. Further, two follow-up visits were scheduled for Day 396 (week 56) and Day 422 (week
60).
Key inclusion criteria include:

Study 05
Study D3461C00005 (05) was a 52-week, multi-center, randomized, double-blind, placebocontrolled, parallel group study. Patients were randomized in a 1:1:1 fashion to anifrolumab
300mg, anifrolumab 150mg, or placebo (Figure 9). Anifrolumab or placebo was administered as

an IV infusion via an infusion pump over a minimum of 30 minutes, every 4 weeks for a total of
13 doses. Randomization was stratified using the following factors: SLEDAI-2K score at
screening (<10 points vs 10 points); week 0 (Day 1) OCS dose (<10 mg/day vs 10 mg/day
prednisone or equivalent); and results of a type I IFN gene signature test (high vs low).
From week 0 (Day 1) to week 12, patients were permitted only 1 burst of corticosteroids for an
increase in SLE disease activity or to control non-SLE related. Patients receiving more than 1
burst during the first 12 weeks of treatment were considered non-responders for subsequent
assessments of disease activity, regardless of the reason for the burst (SLE or non-SLE activity).
Permitted medications for SLE standard of care are described in the protocol. Patients who
discontinued investigational product (IP) were encouraged to continue to undergo all studyrelated visits/procedures for the full treatment period (52 weeks) in order to support the final
efficacy and safety analysis.

Multiplicity control
Study 1013: The testing procedures specified that a global trend test (Cochran-Armitage trend
test) for the 1000 mg and 300 mg doses would be performed in the overall population and the
diagnostic positive subpopulation (i.e., type I IFN test high patients). No multiplicity adjustment
for the two populations was applied and alpha was pre-specified to be 0.10. If the test in the
overall population was significant at 0.10, then each dose would be compared to placebo at a
significance level of 0.10, without specification of order. No other adjustments were planned
for the secondary endpoints.
Study 05: The primary and key secondary endpoints were tested using a weighted Holm
procedure. The procedure applies alpha recycling according to the weights shown in Figure 11

Considerations on the Clinical Endpoints
Primary Endpoints Considerations:
The question of how best to measure treatment response in SLE remains an open one. 25
Current SLE trial endpoints are based on measures of disease activity including the SLEDAI-2K
and BILAG. These disease indices are used to calculate a disease activity score, and trial
endpoints may be determined based on specifying thresholds for improvement. 26 The SLEDAI2K is a composite global disease activity index developed using consensus techniques in which
24 manifestations are scored across nine organ systems and marked as present or absent over
the preceding 30 days according to binary cutoffs. 27 Advantages of the SLEDAI-2K include
construct validity and the ability to predict major outcomes such as organ damage and
mortality. 28 However, drawbacks of the SLEDAI-2K include the possibility of different disease
patterns to result in the same total score, while improvement or worsening in other organ
systems may not be reflected in the score; furthermore, the dichotomous scoring may not
allow for variations in the severity of manifestations, which reduces sensitivity to change when
used to assess treatment response. 29 The BILAG disease activity index is a set of organ-based
scales rather than a composite score; the current version (BILAG-2004) comprises of nine organ
systems with 97 descriptors, and each is scored as new or recurring, worse, same, improving, or
not present during the past 30 days as defined by a glossary. These scores are combined to
assign each organ an A–E grade: A (major activity), B (intermediate activity), C (mild or stable
disease), D (previous involvement but currently inactive), and E (no previous activity). 30 The
BILAG index has undergone extensive testing of reliability and validity for the purpose of
measuring disease activity; however, it was not developed as a measure of treatment response,
and there are limitations to its adaptation for the purpose of a clinical trial endpoint. Although
gradations in severity mean that the BILAG-2004 is more sensitive to change than the SLEDAI2K, 31 predefined discrete thresholds may still limit responsiveness to differences between
treatment arms in a clinical trial. Another potential drawback of the BILAG-2004 is that it is a

trials: a problem worth solving. Lancet Rheumatology. Volume 3, Issue 8, E595-E603, August 01, 2021. Published
Online June 7, 2021; https://doi.org/10.1016/ S2665-9913(21)00119-3.

Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
transitional index requiring comparison of the current state with the previous month to assign
improvement or worsening, so two patients with identical current activity can score differently.
The potential limitations of the individual instruments SLEDAI-2K and BILAG-2004 to serve as
clinical trial endpoints has led to the development of composite indices such as the SRI and the
BICLA, which are currently the most common primary efficacy endpoints used in SLE clinical
trials. An SRI response was originally defined as a reduction in SLEDAI of 4 points or more, with
the requirement of no BILAG worsening (1A or 2Bs) or deterioration from baseline Physician
Global Assessment of 0.3 or more. The SRI was developed on the basis of post-hoc analysis of
data from a phase 2 trial of belimumab and was then used as a successful endpoint in two
phase 3 belimumab trials. 32 Because the SRI responder classification is mostly driven by SLEDAI2K improvement, i.e. a 4 point improvement in SLEDAI-2K score to achieve the SRI-4 endpoint,
it has the potential disadvantage of not being able to detect partial improvements;
furthermore, highly weighted disease manifestations can drive the SLEDAI-2K reduction needed
for an SRI response while lower weighted domains might not result in the threshold needed for
achieving a response. A BICLA response is defined as (1) at least one gradation of improvement
in all BILAG A and B scores present at baseline; (2) no new BILAG A, or 2 or more new BILAG B
scores; (3) no worsening of total SLEDAI-2K score from baseline; and (4) no deterioration in
Physician Global Assessment ( 10 mm on a 100 mm scale). 33 In contrast to the SRI responder
index which requires complete improvement in at least one organ system but not necessarily
improvement in all affected organ systems, the BICLA threshold can be achieved not only by
complete resolution but also by substantial partial improvement in multiple affected organs.
Both SRI-4 and BICLA are considered valid indices, 34 however they may demonstrate discordant
responses with some subjects achieving one but not the other threshold for improvement. This
type of discordance was observed for first phase 3 study of anifrolumab (study 05), in which a
number of subjects in the placebo arm achieved improvement on SRI-4 but not BICLA
(discussed in more detail below). There is also some evidence that the placebo response rates
may be higher with SRI-4 than BICLA. 35
The Applicant provided adequate justification for changing the primary endpoint to BICLA in its
second phase 3 trial from SRI-4, as described above. The Applicant proposed that this endpoint
is more comprehensive, gives more balanced weight to affected body systems, and measure

incremental improvements or worsening. The Applicant also noted that this endpoint retains

BILAG-2004 from being classified as responders. Furthermore, the FDA guidance34 endorses the
use of the BILAG questionnaire stating, “the BILAG is the preferred index to study reduction in
disease activity in clinical trials.” Given the endorsement of BILAG in the SLE guidance, the
choice of the BICLA primary endpoint for the second anifrolumab phase 3 study was deemed
reasonable by the Agency.
Secondary Endpoints: CLASI
The Applicant proposed to use the Cutaneous Lupus Erythematosus Disease Area and Severity
Index (CLASI) as a secondary endpoint measuring response of SLE cutaneous disease activity to
anifrolumab as compared to placebo. 36 In consultation with the Division and Dermatology and
Dentistry, it has been determined that the CLASI is not a fit-for purpose instrument for
(b) (4)
measurement of reduction of skin disease in SLE subjects
and considers its use exploratory based on the
following considerations:
1. The CLASI Total Activity Score is driven by the erythema component of the scale,
with 39 of a possible 70 total points being potentially attainable just from the
erythema score (if each anatomic site received the highest rating of 3; the
likelihood of this occurring is unclear). While the extent of cutaneous
involvement may be reflective of disease activity, it is not clear that the depth of
erythema correlates with the level of activity. Further, the appearance of
erythema may be influenced by the inherent depth of an individual’s
pigmentation, differing in presentation in a fair-skinned person compared to a
darkly-pigmented person. It is unclear how the difference in clinical presentation
of erythema in different skin types might impact scoring of the depth of
erythema. Indeed, erythema in dark skin may even be missed by an untrained
observer.
2. The eruption of acute cutaneous lupus erythematosus (ACLE), e.g. the butterfly
rash of SLE, occurs most frequently during an acute SLE flare. Some authors
describe that the lesions of ACLE “can be quite ephemeral, lasting only hours,
days, or weeks…” Therefore, for some individuals (i.e. those with transitory
lesions), resolution of ACLE may not reflect a positive treatment response, but
may, instead, simply be coincident to treatment.
3. The CLASI Activity scale does not address lesion morphology (other than mucosal
ulcers). For cutaneous lesions other than alopecia, the scale measures three

36

Albrecht J, Taylor L, Berlin JA, Dulay S, Ang G, et al. The CLASI (Cutaneous Lupus Erythematosus Disease Area and
Severity Index): an outcome instrument for cutaneous lupus erythematosus. J Invest Dermatol 125:889 –894,
2005.

71
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
secondary characteristics: erythema on a three-point scale, scaling on a twopoint scale, and verrucosity/hypertrophy as a single point at the extreme for
scaling.
4. The presentations of cutaneous LE are highly variable, and each CLASI score
represents a bundling of different cutaneous signs into a single score. Each
subject’s activity score may represent a different combination of clinical signs.
For example, there are several possible ways for arriving at a Total Activity Score
of, say, “12”: This total could reflect the presence of erythema at several sites; it
could reflect erythema with mucous membrane involvement, erythema with
scale and alopecia, etc. Therefore, a decrease in CLASI might reflect
improvement in different elements of the scale (e.g. improvement in alopecia or
erythema) depending on what elements on the scale contributed to an
individual’s baseline score. Simply reporting a decrease in the CLASI does not
permit or present a clinical picture of the nature of improvement, as one would
not be able to determine what elements on the CLASI were impacted by study
treatment and made for the reduction in score.
Therefore, the review team determined that the CLASI results are not easily interpretable

(b) (4)

(b) (4)

Secondary Endpoints: FACIT-Fatigue
The Applicant proposed to use the FACIT-Fatigue (FACIT-F) as a secondary endpoint measuring
response of SLE patient fatigue to anifrolumab as compared to placebo. 37 In consultation with
Division of Clinical Outcome Assessment, it has been determined that, while fatigue appears to
be an important and common symptom in patients with SLE, the Applicant did not submit
sufficient evidence to support interpretation of FACIT-F scores. It is unclear whether changes in
the FACIT-F total score in the anifrolumab clinical program are clinically meaningful. The
threshold for clinically meaningful within-patient change to assess fatigue severity in the clinical
trials could not be estimated using anchor-based methods as the clinical trials did not include
appropriate anchor scales. Based on the empirical distribution function (eCDF) curves and itemlevel analyses, there does not appear to be a difference in the change from baseline at Week 52
in FACIT-F total scores between treatment arms in the phase 3 studies (see Figure 13). FACIT-F
items have limitations related to content validity that present additional challenges to
interpreting FACIT-F data in the Applicant’s clinical trials.

37

Lai JS, Beaumont JL, Ogale S, Brunetta P, Cella D. Validation of the functional assessment of chronic illness
therapy-fatigue scale in patients with moderately to severely active systemic lupus erythematosus, participating in
a clinical trial. J Rheumatol. 2011;38(4):672-679.

Study Results
Compliance with Good Clinical Practices
According to statements included in the report for the three studies submitted to support
approval (studies 1013, 05, and 04), the Applicant certified that this study was conducted in
compliance with the following: good clinical practice standards as outlined in the Declaration of
Helsinki 2008 or the International Conference on Harmonization (ICH) Good Clinical Practice
(GCP) guidelines and applicable country-specific requirements including the constitution of
independent ethics committees as per 21 CFR 312.3(b).
During the studies (prior to unblinding) it was discovered that one site in each of studies 04 and
05 had sustained non-compliance with protocol procedures, so the sites were closed. A decision
was made and documented in the study SAP to exclude all 11 randomized patients from these 2

Key data from these excluded patients are listed in the respective CSR.
Financial Disclosure
The Applicant completed FDA Form 3454 to document financial conflicts of interest and
arrangements with clinical investigators. As per this form, the Applicant certified that no
financial arrangement had been entered into with the listed clinical investigators, whereby the
value of compensation to the investigator could be affected by the outcome of the study as
defined in 21 CFR 54.2 (a, b, and f).

countries in study 1013 (Table 7). A total of 365 patients in study 04 and 457 patients in study
05 met eligibility criteria and were randomized into the studies at 219 sites in 28 countries
(Table 8).

Protocol Violations/Deviations
A small number of patients had an important protocol deviation (as defined in ICH E3 1995):
14/362 (3.9%) and 18/457 (3.9%) patients in the full analysis sets of studies 04 and 05,
respectively, and 10/307 randomized patients (3.3%) in study 1013. Overall, the most frequent
important protocol deviations were related to the timing of the laboratory procedures for the
PK, investigational product, inclusion criteria, principal investigator
oversight/delegation/training, and ‘other’ reasons. The treatment groups were balanced with
respect to both incidence and type of important protocol deviations. With the exception of the
11 patients from the 2 sites that were closed because of sustained non-compliance with
protocol procedures, no other patients were excluded from the full analysis sets because of
deviations from the protocol. There were no concerns regarding protocol deviations in terms of
study conduct or the safety of patients. Also, the numbers and types of protocol deviations
were judged not to influence the study outcome.
Specific details for protocol deviations are shown in Table 9, Table 10, and Table 11.

Table of Demographic Characteristics
The demographic characteristics of the patients in the 300 mg and placebo arms were broadly
similar across the 3 studies submitted to support approval and are representative of the target
population of patients with moderate to severe SLE despite standard therapy (Table 12). The
mean age was between 39.1 and 43.1 years and the majority of patients were female (91.2% to
93.9%). Approximately 40% of the patients had a BMI of 28 kg/m2. Overall, races were well
represented.
All 3 studies included patients from all geographic regions (Table 12). One of the few
differences between the studies was the geographic placement of the studies:
o Study 04 had a higher proportion of patients from the Asia-Pacific region (14.6%)
compared with studies 05 (5.3%) and 1013 (5.9%) and was the only study to include
patients from Japan.
o Study 05 had a higher proportion of patients from Europe (37.9%) compared with
