o Study 1013 had a higher proportion of patients from Latin America (36.7%) than studies
04 (18.5%) and 05 (13.6%).
o All 3 studies had similar proportions (approximately one-third) of patients from

Other Baseline Characteristics (e.g., disease characteristics, important concomitant drugs)
Baseline SLE disease characteristics
The inclusion or exclusion criteria were consistent across the studies, and the baseline SLE
disease characteristics of the patients were broadly similar and consistent with a moderate to
severe active SLE population (Table 13). At baseline, patients in study 05 had slightly higher
mean PGA scores than patients in study 04, indicative of more active disease, and had more
moderate to severe skin involvement (CLASI activity score 10 and BILAG ‘A’ mucocutaneous
items). In the two phase 3 studies, baseline SLEDAI-2K organ system involvement were also
generally balanced, with the exception of baseline active hematology and immunology higher in
the anifrolumab 300 mg group than placebo in study 04 (Table 14). Other than that, the
baseline disease characteristics were generally balanced between the studies and between
study arms.
In all 3 studies, patients in the anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo arms had moderate to severe
disease activity at baseline, as indicated by mean SLEDAI-2K scores of 10.7 to 11.5, and 59.6%
to 73.4% of patients with a SLEDAI-2K score of 10; mean BILAG-2004 scores of 18.6 to 19.8;
and median PGA scores of 1.68 to 1.87 (Table 13). Overall, 45.0% to 52.5% of patients had
severe disease in at least 1 organ system (BILAG-2004 level A), and 41.4% to 50.6% of patients
had moderate disease in at least 2 organ systems (BILAG-2004 level B). Patients had a median
duration of disease/time since diagnosis of 65.75 to 94.5 months (Table 13).

Referring to organ system components of the SLEDAI-2K, the patients in the 300 mg and
placebo arms in studies 04, 05 and 1013 had multi-organ involvement at baseline with the most
common organ manifestations of SLE disease being musculoskeletal, mucocutaneous and
immunological (Table 14). Based on BILAG-2004 organ items, the most common organ systems
with baseline BILAG A or B severity in all three studies (1013, 04 & 05) in the 300 mg and
placebo arms were mucocutaneous and musculoskeletal (as shown in Table 13 of the Summary
of Clinical Efficacy). Almost all patients had active skin disease (CLASI activity score > 0) with a
mean CLASI score of 6.7 to 8.5, and 22.0% to 32.2% of patients in the 300 mg and placebo arms
had moderate to severe skin disease (CLASI activity score 10 points) (Table 13). Across the
studies, the majority of patients had active joint manifestations, with 88.9% to 92.4% of
patients having swollen joints (median joint counts of 5.0 to 7.0), and 93.3% to 100% of patients
having tender joints (median joint counts of 7.0 to 11.0).
The baseline immunology/serology characteristics were similar across the studies. The majority
of patients were classified as type I IFN gene signature test high at screening (74.5% to 83.3%)
(Table 13). While all patients had to be auto-antibody positive, the baseline results were:
x ANA positivity in most patients (88.9% to 99.0%)
x Anti-dsDNA positivity in 40.1% to 47.8% of patients in studies 04 and 05, and 72.2% to
80.5% of patients in study 1013 (which used the Farr assay, the most specific method for
detecting dsDNA antibodies). Note, that study 1013 also used a multiplex assay, but a
different type (with different cut-off values) to the multiplex assay used in studies 04
and 05.
x Abnormal (low) C3 levels (28.3% to 42.2%)
x Abnormal (low) C4 levels (19.4% to 27.2%)
Baseline SLE-related SOC treatments
All patients were required to be on stable doses of standard of care (SOC) treatments for SLE
for an appropriate period of time prior to entering the study. The baseline disease-related SOC
treatments across the 3 studies were generally similar and balanced between treatment groups
(Table 15 and Table 16).

the 3 studies (Table 15 and Table 16).

Fewer patients in study 04 (48.3% anifrolumab and 45.6% placebo) were taking 10 mg/day
OCS, than in study 05 (57.2% anifrolumab and 55.4% placebo) and study 1013 (55.6%
anifrolumab and 62.7% placebo). However, the majority of patients in the 3 studies were on a
stable OCS dose at baseline. For these patients, the mean dose of OCS was 10.6 mg/day to 12.8
mg/day (Table 16). About two-thirds of patients in studies 04 and 05 were taking an OCS in
combination with immunosuppressants and/or anti-malarials.
Across the studies, 66.1% to 76.8% of patients in the anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo
treatment arms were taking anti-malarials.

Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use
Table 15 and Table 16 describe the baseline use of standard of care treatment. Table 8
describes the disposition of patients who completed/discontinued investigational product for
studies 05 and 04. In study 05, the proportion of patients who completed investigational
product was 80.6% on both anifrolumab 300mg and 150mg, and 79.3% on placebo. In study 04,
the proportion of patients who completed investigational product was 85.0% on anifrolumab
300mg and 71.4% on placebo. For study 1013, the proportion of patients who completed
investigational product was 73.1% on anifrolumab 1000mg, 87.9% on anifrolumab 300mg, and
69.6% on placebo.
In study 1013, the number of patients who exceeded OCS after randomization was 14 (13.5%)
on anifrolumab 1000mg, 18 (18.2%) on anifrolumab 300mg, and 15 (14.7%) on placebo. In
study 05, under the pre-specified set of restricted medication rules, the number of patients
who initiated restricted medication after randomization was 66 (36.7%) on anifrolumab 300mg,
36 (38.7%) on anifrolumab 150mg, and 70 (38.0%) on placebo. Under the study 04 set of
restricted medication rules, the number of patients who initiated restricted medication after
randomization was 40 (22.2%) on anifrolumab 300mg, 21 (22.6%) on anifrolumab 150mg, and
56 (30.4%) on placebo. In study 04, the number of patients who initiated restricted medication
after randomization was 36 (20.0%) on anifrolumab 300mg, and 59 (32.4%) on placebo. We
note these patients were classified as non-responders in endpoints that pre-specifed the
prohibition of such initiation of restricted medication.

Table 18 and Table 19 below summarize data capture for study 05 and 04, respectively, at week
52. Both studies had approximately 14-15% missing data at week 52. The Applicant prespecified that missing week 52 data, either complete or partial (i.e., missing one or more items
within a component) would be imputed with week 48 data, if available. This is indicated by
“based on imputation” in the following tables. If week 48 data was not available, the patient
was to be classified as a non-responder. Compared to Table 8 (the Applicants tabulations),
there are minor discrepancies in the number of patients who completed week 52. This is due to
patients being labeled as having completed week 52 by the Applicant, however, they have 1 or
more missing components (hence labeled as missing by the statistical reviewer), or patients not
being considered as having completed week 52 by the Applicant, but had week 52 data
imputed via week 48 data.

Study 1013
The pre-specified analysis of the primary endpoint of SRI-4 + OCS tapering at week 24 was
conducted in the whole population and in the subset of type I IFN test high patients. As alpha
was prespecified at 0.10, the odds ratio and corresponding 90% confidence intervals are
reported comparing anifrolumab 1000 mg and 300 mg to placebo. To aid in interpretability with
study 05 and 04, the 95% confidence intervals are shown. The results were significant in the
overall population and in the subset of type I IFN test high patients based on the pre-specified
alpha of 0.10, as the p-values from the Cochran-Armitage global trend test were 0.07 and 0.03
respectively. However, no adjustments were made for the evaluation of the endpoint in two
populations. The pairwise comparisons of each dose to placebo for the overall population and
the subset of type I IFN test high patients are reported in Table 20.

While the Applicant’s pre-specified significance level was 0.10, all further analyses and results
are presented and discussed using an alpha of 0.05 to aid in interpretability. Further, as the
Applicant did not pre-specify any multiplicity adjustments for the secondary endpoints and
some analyses are post-hoc, if appropriate, results are referred to as nominally significant.

Table 21 below displays the results for the difference in proportions of SRI-4 + OCS tapering and

discontinuation and excess use of immunosuppressants and OCS tapering requirements at

requirements are not necessarily considered treatment failures. Regardless of excessive OCS
use or OCS tapering requirements, anifrolumab 300 mg was nominally significant when
compared to placebo.

Site Inspection Concerns
Concern arose after site inspections over sites 7815 and 7834 in study 04. A sensitivity analysis
was performed on the primary endpoint with patients from these sites excluded from the
analysis. Site 7815 had 10 patients and site 7834 had 7 patients, so 17 patients were removed
from the analysis. The results are summarized in Table 29 below. Excluding the sites had little
impact and the conclusion remains unchanged. Therefore, there is no concern on the impact of
removing the sites on the analysis of the primary endpoint.

Sensitivity Analyses
A tipping point analysis was performed to assess the impact of missing data. This analysis
considered the number of patients on placebo and anifrolumab with missing data and varies
the possible number of responses among these missing outcomes on the two treatment arms.
The goal is to explore the plausibility of scenarios under which the conclusions change, i.e.,
under which there is no longer evidence of a treatment effect. For a fixed number of additional
responders on anifrolumab, we consider how many additional responders on placebo it will
take to observe a p-value less than 0.05 to greater 0.05. For example, if the assumption is that
there is only 1 additional responder among the missing outcomes on anifrolumab 300mg, the
analysis can be used to explore how many additional responders on placebo it would take to tip
the results. The plausibility of the scenarios under which conclusions may be tipped can then be
assessed.
Further, we make use of the of the knowledge of whether patients exceeded/initialized
restricted medication before the endpoint. By definition of the primary endpoint, such patients
were considered non-responders, regardless of how favorable the efficacy outcomes were.
Likewise, patients with missing data, but who were known to have exceeded/initialized
restricted medication are considered non-responders in the tipping point analysis. By taking this
approach, this greatly reduced the number of patients with uncertain response status.
However, it should be noted that targeting the treatment policy estimand, which would ignore
prior use of excess/initialized restricted medication, would lead to a greater number of patients
with unknown response status, and thus the results will be less robust.
Tipping point analyses were performed for all 3 studies on the analysis of SRI-4 and BICLA. As
anifrolumab 150 mg and anifrolumab 1000 mg are not being considered for approval, the
tipping point analyses only considered comparisons between the 300 mg arm and placebo.
Further, to explore all potential effects of missing data, in these analyses, patients who were
considered as having available week 52 data based on week 48 LOCF data, are considered as
missing.
Study 1013
SRI-4 + OCS tapering at week 24

Study 05
BICLA
Tipping point analyses were performed using the results for BICLA response under the post-hoc
study 04 restricted medication rules, since it is considered to be more clinically relevant and
what will appear on the label.
There were 41 patients on the anifrolumab 300mg arm and 45 patients on the placebo arm that
had missing week 52 data. Of these, 35 initiated restricted medication (based on study 04 rules)
and are considered non-responders for the tipping point analyses. Therefore, the tipping point
analyses considers the 51 patients, 26 on anifrolumab 300mg, and 25 on placebo, as having
missing data.
Table 32 below displays the number of additional placebo responders needed to tip results per
additional number of added responders on anifrolumab. For example, given 0 additional
responders on anifrolumab 300mg, it would require at least 13 additional responders on
placebo.
Based on placebo completers, an estimated response rate is 36.0%, and the estimated response
rate among anifrolumab completers in this study is 56.8%. All scenarios that result in tipping
the conclusions would require at least 52% of the placebo subjects with missing data to be
responders. If we further consider that at least some of the 26 missing anifrolumab 300mg
subjects could be responders, based on these observed rates, an even higher response rate
would be required among missing placebo subjects to tip the results. Considering the higher
proportion of missing placebo subjects required to be responders to tip the results relative to
the observed rates among placebo completers, we consider this to be a relatively unlikely
scenario.

Table 35 displays the results on BILAG flare rate through week 52. A flare is defined as either 1
or more new A or 2 or more new B items compared to the previous visit. The rate ratio
comparing anifrolumab 1000mg and 300mg to placebo was 0.67 and 0.72, respectively. Since
the rate ratio is defined as the ratio of the rate of anifrolumab over the rate of placebo, this
means that a rate ratio of < 1 favors anifrolumab in reducing BILAG flares. However, since the
95% confidence intervals includes 1, we conclude that anifrolumab 1000mg and 300mg are not
supportive in having more patients reduce annual flare rate compared to placebo.

Study 05
Original Restricted Medication Rules
This section presents the results for the pre-specified key secondary endpoints under the prespecified set of restricted medication rules. Since the primary endpoint failed, any positive
results were considered nominally significant.
Table 36 display the results of the following pre-specified secondary endpoints: maintain OCS
reduction at week 52, CLASI response at week 12, SRI-4 response in type I IFN test high patients
at week 52, and SRI-4 response at week 24.
Among patients whose baseline OCS use was 10 mg/day, maintaining OCS reduction was
measured by the ability to reduce OCS use from 10 mg/day at baseline to 7.5mg/day by week
40 and maintaining 7.5mg/day through week 52. The difference in proportions comparing
anifrolumab 300mg and 150mg to placebo was 8.9 and 4.5, respectively. Neither anifrolumab
300mg or 150mg were supportive in having more patients achieve OCS reduction compared to
placebo.
Among patients with CLASI activity score 10 at baseline, CLASI response was measured by the
ability to achieve 50% reduction in CLASI activity score at week 12. The difference in
proportions comparing anifrolumab 300mg and 150mg to placebo is 17.0 and 26.2,
respectively. We see that anifrolumab 300mg is not supportive in having more patients achieve
50% reduction in CLASI activity score at week 12 compared to placebo.
Among patients in the type I IFN test high subgroup, for SRI-4 response at week 52, the
difference in proportions in responders comparing anifrolumab 300mg and 150mg to placebo is
-3.4 and 0.4, respectively. We see that neither dose was supportive in having more patients
achieve SRI-4 response compared to placebo.
For SRI-4 at week 24, the difference in proportions in responders comparing anifrolumab
300mg and 150mg to placebo is 0.1 and -4.7, respectively. We see that neither dose is
supportive in having more patients achieve SRI-4 response at week 24 compared to placebo.

Subgroups
This section summarizes results from various subgroup analyses for the primary endpoint,
BICLA response at week 52 within subgroup levels. The subgroups and levels explored are:

BICLA response at week 52 is considered for each study. We further performed shrinkage
analyses for sex, age, race, and region. Bayesian hierarchical modeling produces shrinkage
estimates of the individual study treatment effects by removing the within study variability.
Further, treatment effects are regarded as exchangeable, which allows them to be different but
related. Therefore, shrinkage estimates tend to be more precise and provide narrower
confidence/credible intervals. Below is the model used:
(‫݌‬஺෣
െ ‫݌‬௣ )௜ ~ ܰ൫(‫݌‬஺ െ ‫݌‬௣ )௜ , ߪ௜ଶ ൯, ݅ = 1, 2
(‫݌‬஺ െ ‫݌‬௣ )௜ ~ ܰ(‫݌‬஺ െ ‫݌‬௣ , ߬ ିଶ ) , ݅ = 1, 2
‫݌‬஺ െ ‫݌‬௣ ~ N(0, 2), ߬ ିଶ ~ ‫(ܰ ܽ݉݉ܽܩ‬0.001, 0.001)
We assume that before seeing data, the difference in proportions is 0, and we further place
one-eighth of a response (BICLA) and one-eighth of a non-response on anifrolumab 300mg and
placebo, which will not be very impactful in linking the groups together. Thus,
ܸܽ‫ݎ‬൫‫݌‬஺ െ ‫݌‬௣ ൯ =

Study 1013
Figure 14 display the forest plots of the subgroup analyses (in isolation and shrinkage) for sex,
age, race, and region. Consistent treatment effects were observed across each of the
subgroups. Among males and patients over 45, the nominal 95% confidence intervals include 0
for both the sample and shrinkage analyses. This is not surprising since the study was not
powered for any of the subgroup analyses and relatively few patients were males.

age, race, and region for study 04. Consistent treatment effects were observed across each of
the subgroups, with the exception that among males, for which the difference in proportions
between was 0. When analyzed in isolation, among males, whites, and in North America, the
nominal 95% confidence intervals include 0. However, after performing the shrinkage analyses,
among patients in the subgroup of white, and in North America, the confidence intervals
exclude 0. The sample size for male subjects is extremely small in this study, making it
challenging to draw conclusions based on the analysis of this subgroup alone.

Dose/Dose Response
Figure 17 below shows the change in SLEDAI-2K, a measure of disease activity that is used to
define both SRI-4 and BICLA, over time from baseline for study 05. The placebo group had the
smallest reduction, followed by anifrolumab 150mg, while anifrolumab 300mg had the largest
reduction throughout all timepoints beginning at week 8 throughout the remainder of the
study. While study 05 failed on the primary endpoint of SRI-4, both under the original and
revised set of study 04 restricted medication rules, and while the differences in SLEDAI-2K
relative to placebo are not statistically significant, these results support a dose-dependent
trend.

Assessment of Efficacy Across Trials
Evaluation of efficacy was evaluated as stand-alone trials. Pooled efficacy analyses were not
considered during this review.
Additional Efficacy Considerations
The Applicant was asked to provide an explanation for the apparent discrepancy between SRI-4
and BICLA responses within study 05 and between study 04 and study 05. These two studies
were identical in design and enrolled patients had similar demographics, baseline SLE disease
characteristics and background medication use (see above Table 12, Table 13, Table 14, and
Table 16). However, while study 04 showed differences of anifrolumab over placebo for both
BICLA and SRI-4 endpoints (Table 27 and Table 28), study 05 only showed differences using the
BICLA endpoint (Table 25 and Table 26).
In order to further understand the discrepant result on SRI(4) in study 05, the Applicant
performed an exploratory post-hoc analysis to compare each patient’s outcome on SRI(4) and
BICLA for each patient in the studies, with the following findings.
x

The majority of patients in both studies (84% to 85%) had concordant outcomes on SRI(4)
and BICLA (i.e., had a SRI[4] response and BICLA response, or a SRI[4] nonresponse and
BICLA nonresponse), and the pattern of concordance was consistent across the studies.


x

In study 05, the difference in the proportion of patients who were both SRI[4]
responders and BICLA responders (‘dual responders’) between anifrolumab 300 mg
and placebo was 14.5% (95% CI (4.6, 24.0)), which is clinically meaningful and similar
to that observed in study 04 [16.9%; 95% CI (7.2, 26.7)].

A smaller proportion of patients in both studies had discordant outcomes on SRI(4) and
BICLA (i.e., had a SRI[4] response and BICLA nonresponse, or a SRI[4] nonresponse and
BICLA response). In one subset in study 05 (SRI[4] responders/BICLA nonresponders;
11.0% of the total patients in the study), more patients in the placebo group were able to
achieve a SRI(4) response than patients in the anifrolumab group (15.2% vs 6.7%), and this
appears to be driving the overall discrepant result on SRI(4) in the study.


In this discordant subset in study 05, patients in the placebo group had lower baseline
disease severity, and greater steroid use during the study, compared with the
anifrolumab 300 mg group. This may have led to more patients in the placebo group
achieving complete resolution of arthritis, which alone meets the criteria for a SRI(4)
response, leading to more SRI(4) responders in the placebo group compared with the
anifrolumab 300 mg group.

108
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE


Despite achieving a SRI(4) response, all the patients in this discordant subset in study
05 were BICLA nonresponders. To be a BICLA responder, a patient must have
improvement in all impacted organ systems at baseline and, as the patients in this
subset had several involved clinical manifestations at baseline and none of them
achieved improvement in all of them, they were all BICLA nonresponders.

We note that these results should be interpreted with caution as they are from exploratory
analyses and in some cases based on a subgroup of patients defined by post-randomization
variables. There was a clinically meaningful difference between the treatment groups in the
proportion of patients who were both a SRI(4) responder and BICLA responder, in favor of
anifrolumab, in both studies. These dual responders, based on 2 validated endpoints, confirm
the beneficial effect of anifrolumab on overall disease activity in patients with SLE. One subset
of patients in study 05 appears to be driving the overall discrepant result on SRI(4) in study 05.
In this subset, more patients in the placebo group were able to achieve a SRI(4) response than
patients in the anifrolumab group. This may be due to an imbalance between the treatment
groups in baseline disease severity, and the degree of steroid use during the study, leading to
more patients in the placebo group achieving complete resolution of arthritis, which alone
meets the criteria for a SRI(4) response.
Integrated Assessment of Effectiveness
Study 04 represents an adequate and well-controlled trial showing favorable and statistically
significant changes on the pre-specified, clinically meaningful primary and secondary endpoints
when comparing anifrolumab 300 mg IV versus placebo. Because the phase 2 trial (1013) was
designed only as a proof of concept and dose-ranging study with inadequate multiplicity control
and study 05, the first phase 3 trial, failed on its primary endpoint, these cannot be considered
additional successful adequate and well-controlled trials when considered independently.
However, the review team feels that when these trials are considered in combination, the
consistent results observed in studies 1013 and 05 do provide confirmatory evidence that
support the effectiveness of anifrolumab 300 mg IV.
Across the three studies, there are three randomized comparisons, each with adequate
duration (52 weeks), that support the claim that anifrolumab 300 mg IV yields higher BICLA
response rates compared to placebo. Further, two of the three studies provide support that
anifrolumab 300 mg IV yields higher SRI-4 response rates compared to placebo at week 52.
While study 05 failed to show an effect on SRI-4, the study showed a numerically dosedependent trend that is not statistically significant in SLEDAI-2K, a measure of disease activity
that is used to define both SRI-4 and BICLA and for which there was relatively consistent
evidence across all three studies. In all three studies, additional supportive evidence was noted
in the analyses of the secondary endpoints, including a greater proportion of subjects on
anifrolumab than placebo able to reduce oral corticosteroid use and a lower proportion of
subjects on anifrolumab experiencing a flare of SLE disease activity, further supporting the
effectiveness of anifrolumab 300 mg IV.

normally non-pathogenic or rarely pathogenic in individuals

Herpes Zoster

A viral infection characterized by a cutaneous vesicular
eruption on an erythematous base presenting along
dermatome(s) and usually associated with prodromal pain.

AEs of herpes zoster included serious
and non-serious AEs identified by select
PTs from the HLT Herpes viral
infections.

TB (including
latent TB)

An active TB infection could be either new or a reactivation
of dormant disease and could be either bacteriologically
confirmed or clinically diagnosed. Latent TB was defined as a
mycobacterial infection without clinical, bacteriological
findings, or radiologic findings consistent with active TB and
a TB blood test using an IGRA or using purified protein
derivative skin test that is positive both at the time of
provisional diagnosis and on repeat assessment.

AEs of TB or latent TB were identified
using a custom MedDRA PT grouping
(PTs: latent tuberculosis, tuberculosis,
and mycobacterium tuberculosis
complex test positive).

Influenza

Where a patient reported a viral infection severe enough to
be considered, a viral test should have been performed to
confirm the diagnosis.
However, if in the opinion of the investigator, the subject
had influenza based on defined clinical symptoms, this was
to be reported as influenza, whether or not a test to confirm
the diagnosis had been performed.

AEs with the PT influenza.
SAEs of influenza were also included in
the AESI category non-opportunistic
serious infection.

Anaphylaxis

A definition based on Sampson et al 2006 was detailed in
Appendix Y to the study 04 and 05 CSPs as a simple and
rapid means to make the diagnosis of anaphylaxis
associated with the infusion of IP.

Custom MedDRA PT grouping (PTs:
anaphylactic reaction, anaphylactic
shock, anaphylactoid reaction, and
anaphylactoid shock).

Hypersensitivity

An acute onset of an illness with involvement of the skin,
mucosal tissue, or both during infusion of IP, but did not
meet the definition of anaphylaxis (i.e., there was no
respiratory compromise or reduced BP/associated
symptoms of end organ dysfunction).

Custom MedDRA PT grouping (LLT
hypersensitivity reaction and PT
hypersensitivity).

Infusion-related
reaction

Infusion-related reaction was defined as any other reaction
(aside from anaphylaxis or hypersensitivity) occurring during
infusion of IP or felt to be temporally related to the infusion
within 24 hours of IP administration.

AEs with the PT infusion-related
reaction.

Malignancy

A neoplasm characterized by cells with abnormal features
and uncontrolled rapid growth with invasive and/or
metastatic tendencies, diagnosed based on pathologic and
clinical standards. Investigators were requested to
additionally obtain biopsy results and pertinent biomarker
and/or genetic testing results.

Four SMQs: hematological malignant
tumors, non-hematological malignant
tumors, malignant lymphomas
(narrow), and skin malignant tumors
(narrow).

MACE

An independent CV-EAC reviewed cases of interest to
determine if they met accepted diagnostic criteria for MACE
events.

For the phase III studies, MACE were
those assessed by the CV-EAC. For
study 1013, MACE were identified using
a custom MedDRA term list.

Review of the Safety Database
Overall Exposure
In the entire clinical development program for anifrolumab, which includes scleroderma and
lupus nephritis in addition to SLE, more than 1,000 subjects have been exposed to at least one
dose of anifrolumab (doses ranging from 150 mg to 1000 mg IV). For SLE specifically, 837 SLE
patients (Table 44) have received at least one dose of anifrolumab (doses ranging from 150 mg
to 1000 mg). The proposed dose for SLE is 300 mg IV every 4 weeks. For the proposed dose of
300 mg in SLE patients, a total of 639 subjects have received at least one dose (Table 45), of
which 180 were initially treated with placebo during the 52-week placebo-controlled periods of
studies 1013, 04, and 05 and then switched to anifrolumab 300 mg in study 1145 (open-label
extension study from 1013) or study 09 (long-term extension study from studies 04 and 05).

Duration of Exposure
In the primary safety pool (phase 3 studies only), patients in the anifrolumab 300 mg group (N =
360) had a total of 326.0 PY of exposure to IP (Table 46). The mean duration of exposure was
330.8 days (SD: 86.24, range: 3 to 419) in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and 319.1 days (SD:
93.41, range: 21 to 393) in the placebo group. Overall, 326 patients (90.6%) were treated with
anifrolumab for 24 weeks and 299 patients (83.1%) were treated with anifrolumab for 48
weeks in the primary safety pool. In the supportive safety pool (studies 1013, 04 & 05), patients
in the anifrolumab 300 mg group (N = 459) had a total of 419.4 PY of exposure to IP (Table 46).
There were 420 patients (91.5%) treated with anifrolumab for 24 weeks and 387 patients
(84.3%) treated with anifrolumab for 48 weeks.

Adequacy of the safety database:
Regarding overall exposure for the assessment of safety, a total of 1029 subjects have been
exposed to any dose of anifrolumab for periods of up to 4 years in the anifrolumab clinical
development program. Of those 1029 subjects, at least 837 were SLE patients exposed to IV
anifrolumab Q4W (150 mg, 300 mg, or 1000 mg). Of these, 688 patients with SLE were exposed
to IV anifrolumab for at least one year and 263 patients were exposed for at least 3 years. 459
subjects were exposed to at least one dose of anifrolumab 300 mg during the 52-week,
placebo-controlled periods of studies 1013, 04 and 05; of those, 420 (91.5%) of subjects were
exposed to 300 mg IV anifrolumab for at least 6 months, and 315 (68.6%) were exposed to 300
mg IV anifrolumab for at least one year. Data from the ongoing long-term extension study (09)
for > 150 subjects exposed to anifrolumab 300 mg for at least 3 years provides additional longterm safety information to support the BLA. Patients who received placebo in the phase 3
studies and enrolled in study 09 were randomized to either continue placebo or receive 300 mg
of anifrolumab. The size of the safety database for this submission is adequate to provide
sufficient basis for a risk/benefit assessment in the adult SLE population.

Adequacy of Applicant’s Clinical Safety Assessments
Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
The data quality submitted was well-organized and adequate to perform a complete review of
safety. Several information requests were sent to the Applicant during the review of safety for
additional analyses which were satisfactory and received in a timely manner.
Categorization of Adverse Events
Verbatim terms of AEs and Disease Related Events (DRE) recorded in the case report forms
(CRF) by investigators were coded by the Applicant using MedDRA dictionary Preferred Term
(PT), High-Level Term (HLT), and System Organ Class (SOC) version 22.1. The MedDRA coding of
the information generated from studies 1013, 1145, 04, 05, and 09 conducted by the Applicant
was generally acceptable. Additionally, the clinical lab and vital sign ranges for clinically
significant abnormal results was reviewed and appeared to be appropriate.
Routine Clinical Tests
The following clinical and lab testing were included in studies 1013, 04, 05 and 09 in support of
anifrolumab IV’s safety profile:
o Symptom driven physical exam, height, and weight
o Vital signs: systolic and diastolic blood pressure, heart rate and temperature
o Complete cell count (CBC) with differential and platelet count, hemoglobin, and
hematocrit
o Serum chemistries: albumin, alkaline phosphatase, ALT, AST, BUN, calcium, chloride,
creatinine, creatine kinase, glucose, gamma glutamyl transferase, potassium, sodium,
total bilirubin, cholesterol, and fasting lipid profile (HDL, LDL, triglycerides)
o Urinalysis: including specific gravity, spot urine protein, spot urine creatinine, and
protein/creatinine ratio
o Pregnancy test
o TB blood test
o Immunology profile: Anti-dsDNA, C3, C4, CH50
o Immunogenicity test (pre-dose)
o 12-lead ECG: baseline and end of study
o Columbia-Suicide Severity Rating Scale (C-SSRS)
Safety Results
Deaths
Overall, there were 14 deaths during the anifrolumab clinical studies in the anifrolumab
development program for SLE including all doses for 52 weeks (studies 1013, 05 and 04) and
placebo controlled long-term extension study 09 (participants in studies 05 and 04 continuing

on either anifrolumab or placebo for greater than 52 weeks). Key patient data for patients with

for completed studies, in the 120-day safety update for the phase 3 long-term extension study
(study 09, LTE), and in an April 2021 response to query regarding the DSUR covering January
2020-2021. No deaths occurred during the phase 2 studies 02 and 08 or during phase 1 studies
(MI-CP180 and 06). Four deaths occurred during the phase 2 and 3 52-week placebo controlled
studies (1013, 04 and 05)—one in the anifrolumab 1000 mg arm of study 1013, one in the
anifrolumab 300 mg arm of study 04, one in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm of study 05, and one
in the placebo arm of study 05. There was one death during the open-label extension (OLE) of
the phase 2 study (1145) in a subject originally randomized to placebo in study 1013 and who
received first 1000 mg anifrolumab (16 doses) and then 300 mg anifrolumab (16 doses) during
the OLE period. For the phase 3 LTE through August 1, 2019 with the initial BLA filing, there
were two deaths, both in the placebo group. Between August 2019 and March 19, 2020, two
additional deaths were recorded in the 120-day safety update of the phase 3 LTE; both in the
anifrolumab 300 mg arm, including one attributed to infection (pneumonia). Five additional
deaths were reported for study 09 in an Annual Report/DSUR for study 09 covering January
2020 through January 2021, all in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm, four attributed to infections (1
pneumonia and 3 COVID-19) and one attributed to SLE exacerbation.

Overall, 11 patients in the all anifrolumab group and 3 patients in the placebo group had a fatal
adverse event. Many of the deaths occurred during the long-term extension study from the two
phase 3 studies (study 09), thus exposure time to anifrolumab was significantly greater than
placebo, resulting in overall similar exposure-adjusted incidence rate (EAIR) for deaths. The
mortality EAIR for anifrolumab was 0.3/100 PY compared to 0.6/100 PY (data not shown). A
number of deaths in the all anifrolumab group were associated with a serious infection,
including 5 due to pneumonia and 3 COVID-19, as compared to one (encephalitis) in the
placebo group, as summarized in Table 49. Limiting the data to only subjects who were either
on anifrolumab IV 300 mg vs placebo for 52+ weeks, there were 7 deaths attributable to
infections (4 pneumonia and 3 COVID-19).

Serious Adverse Events
Serious adverse events: 52-week treatment period
In a pool of the three studies submitted to support approval (1013, 04 and 05), fewer patients
in the anifrolumab 300 mg group had any Serious Adverse Event (SAE) than patients in the
placebo group (Table 50; 11.8% vs 16.7%); the EAIR risk difference was -7.2 (95% CI -12.5, -1.9).
Similarly, in a pool limited to the two phase 3 studies (04 and 05), fewer patients in the
anifrolumab 300 mg group had any Serious Adverse Event (SAE) than patients in the placebo
group (11.1% vs 16.4%); the EAIR risk difference was -7.3 (95% CI: -13.3, -1.4). Among patients
with SAEs during the 52-week treatment period, in both treatment groups, the most common
SAEs by PT were pneumonia and systemic lupus erythematosus.
121
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE

There were 8 patients (1.7%) in the anifrolumab group and 9 patients (1.9%) in the placebo
group who had an SAE of pneumonia (EAIR 1.9 vs 2.2; risk difference -0.3, 95% CI -2.5, 1.8).
Among the 8 SAEs on pneumonia in the anifrolumab 300 mg group, two were fatal (see details
above) and there were no fatal pneumonias in the placebo group.
As a safety variable, the PT of systemic lupus erythematosus included SLE flares, where subjects
experienced increased disease activity with new or worsening clinical signs and symptoms.
There were 7 patients (1.5%) in the anifrolumab group and 14 patients (3.0%) in the placebo
group who had an SAE of systemic lupus erythematosus (EAIR 0.7 vs 3.5; risk difference -1.8,
95% CI (-4.3, 0.4).
One case of SAE-hypersensitivity and two cases of SAE-angioedema occurred during treatment
with anifrolumab 300 mg for a total of 0.6% (3/459) of patients experiencing hypersensitivity
events during the 52-week clinical trial periods of the three studies submitted for approval.
There was one event of anaphylactic reaction reported in the SLE development program in a
subject receiving anifrolumab 150 mg (study 05).
(b) (6)

In the one reported case of anaphylaxis with anifrolumab - patient
in study
D3461C00005 - a 27year old white female from Argentina experienced a mild pruritic rash after
the first dose of study treatment with anifrolumab 150 mg infusion. The patient was
prophylactically administered one dose of diphenhydramine prior to the second infusion. On
the day of Dose 2, during the post-dose flush infusion, the patient developed anaphylactic
reaction with dyspnea, wheezing, flushing, and pruritis; was treated with hydrocortisone,
diphenhydramine, and epinephrine; and was hospitalized for anaphylactic reaction. Both the
initial pruritic rash and anaphylactic reaction were considered to be related to study treatment
with anifrolumab.

Serious adverse events: Phase 3 long-term extension treatment period (data not shown)
The phase 3 LTE group (study 09, including 120-day safety update) was similar to the 52-week
treatment period, where SAEs were lower in the anifrolumab 300 mg group than the placebo
group: 21.7% vs 22.2%; EAIR 10.1 vs 17.2 (risk difference -7.1; 95% CI -11.2, -3.2). Also
consistent with the 52-week safety pool, the most common SAEs in both treatment groups in
the phase 3 LTE period were pneumonia and systemic lupus erythematosus. Pneumonia was
balanced between the two groups (9 (2.5%) in anifrolumab vs 10 (2.7%) in placebo (EAIR 1.0 vs
1.9; difference -0.9, 95% CI -2.6, 0.4)). Systemic lupus erythematosus flare/worsening SAE was
less common in the anifrolumab group as compared to the placebo group (6 (1.7%) in
anifrolumab vs 15 (4.1%) in placebo (EAIR 0.7 vs 2.9; difference -2.2, 95% CI -4.1, -0.9)). During
up to 4 years of treatment, the EAIR of systemic lupus erythematosus SAEs was lower in the
anifrolumab 300 mg group than the placebo group.
The evaluation of SAEs by yearly intervals did not suggest any change in overall SAE incidence of
the course of long-term treatment. Evaluation of the most common SAEs in the anifrolumab
300 mg group by yearly intervals showed similar event rates over time (Table 51).

Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to Adverse Effects
Overall, in the 52-week treatment period in the three studies submitted to support approval as
well as in the phase 3 long-term extension data, there was low incidence of discontinuation
from investigational product (IP) due to adverse event/s that was similar across treatment
groups (Table 52). The proportion of patients with an adverse event leading to discontinuation
of IP (DAEs) was low and favored the anifrolumab 300 mg group (4.1%) over the placebo group
(5.2%); EAIR 4.5 vs 6.0 (risk difference -1.4 (95% CI -4.7, 1.7). The most commonly reported
DAEs by SOC in the anifrolumab group were Infections and Infestations with no PT
predominating; the most commonly reported DAEs by SOC in the placebo group were
Musculoskeletal and connective tissue disorders all in PT of systemic lupus erythematosus
(flare). There was no evidence for an increase in rate of discontinuations over time in the phase
3 long-term safety data. As observed during the 52-week treatment period, rates of DAEs in the
phase 3 long-term safety data (including 120-day safety update) were low and similar between
the anifrolumab 300 mg group (EAIR 2.6/100 PY) and placebo group (4.2/100 PY) (data not
shown).

Increased Incidence in anifrolumab 300 mg of DAEs in the SOC group nervous system
disorders
The second most commonly reported DAE by SOC in the anifrolumab group were Nervous
system disorders with no PT predominating (Table 52)—5 (1.1%) cases in the anifrolumab 300
mg group vs 1 (0.2%) case in the placebo group; EAIR 1.2 vs 0.2 (risk difference 0.9; 95% CI -0.3,
2.5). In the phase 3 long-term extension pool (including 120-day safety update), which included
only subjects receiving anifrolumab 300 mg or placebo for both their initial 52-week treatment
period as well as long-term extension period up to 4 years, there was a similar imbalance of
DAEs with 6 DAEs in the SOC of nervous system disorders in the anifrolumab group and 0 in the
placebo group, again with no PT predominating (Table 53).

Two of the 3 studies submitted to support approval provide potential data on dose-relatedness
for DAEs. In study 05, the 150 mg dose of anifrolumab was studied in parallel to the 300 mg
dose in addition to the placebo arm. One patient in the placebo arm of study 05 was recorded
with a DAE for SOC of nervous system disorders (PT neuropsychiatric lupus), while 3 were
recorded in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm (no PT predominating) and 0 recorded in the
anifrolumab 150 mg arm. In the phase 2 study/1013, 1 DAE for SOC of nervous system disorders
was recorded in the placebo arm (optic neuritis) and 1 DAE for SOC of nervous system disorders
was recorded in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm (transverse myelitis); 0 DAE for SOC of nervous
system disorders recorded in the anifrolumab 1000 mg arm.
For total treatment-emergent adverse events, the SOC of nervous system disorders was
common in both placebo and anifrolumab treatment arms (ranging from 14-31%), with
headache as the predominating PT. 52-week data from studies 1013, 04 and 05 showed an
imbalance with SOC nervous system disorders occurring in 19.4% (EAIR 23.9) of anifrolumab
300 mg subjects and 16.7% (EAIR 21.7) of placebo subjects. The phase 3 LTE data (including
120-day safety update) also showed an imbalance with SOC nervous system disorders occurring
in 31.1% of anifrolumab 300 mg subjects (EAIR 16.6) and 18.1% of placebo subjects (EAIR 14.5).
In study 05, TEAE for SOC nervous system disorders was higher in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm
(23.3%; EAIR 26.1) than the anifrolumab 150 mg arm (14%; EAIR 15.6); placebo arm was lower
than anifrolumab 300 mg arm but higher than the anifrolumab 150 mg arm (14%, EAIR 19.7).
Study 1013 did not show an imbalance between study arms for SOC nervous system
disorders—24% (EAIR 34.3) in the placebo group; 21% in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm; 20% in
the anifrolumab 1000 mg arm (EAIR 26.2).
Serious adverse events (SAE) for the SOC of nervous system disorders were uncommon (<2%)
for any of the anifrolumab treatment doses during the 52-week treatment period. For the 300
mg dose of anifrolumab during 52-weeks, SAEs were balanced between anifrolumab and
placebo groups—4 (0.9%)/EAIR 1.0 vs 5 (1.0%)/EAIR 1.3. A higher number of SAE for SOC
nervous system disorders occurred in the anifrolumab 300 mg group than the placebo group in
the phase 3 long-term safety data—7 (1.9%)/EAIR 0.8 vs 1 (0.3%)/EAIR 0.2. The 7 SAEs included
2 cases of post-herpetic neuralgia and 1 case each of hypoesthesia, intracranial aneurysm,
ischemic stroke, myasthenia gravis, and syncope.

126
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
The SAE/DAE of myasthenia gravis occurred during study 05 in a 67 year old female 22 days
after her fifth dose of anifrolumab 300 mg. The SAE/DAE of transverse myelitis occurred during
study 1013 in a 51 year old female 3 days after her second dose of anifrolumab 300 mg; this
patient was found to have positive CSF PCR for varicella and the transverse myelitis was
ultimately attributed to Herpes Zoster (and resolved after treatment with steroids and
acyclovir). Two cases of SAE/DAE post-herpetic neuralgia occurred during the long-term
extension study. Thus, 3 SAE/DAE cases for anifrolumab 300 mg in the SOC of nervous system
disorders were directly associated with Herpes Zoster infection (1 in 52-week data, 2 in longterm extension data).
Significant Adverse Events
Table 54 is a tabular listing of adverse events observed during the 52-week study period
(studies 1013, 04 and 05) by treatment group that were rated as severe by study investigators.
A slightly higher proportion of subjects in the anifrolumab 300 mg group experienced severe
treatment emergent adverse events as compared to the placebo group—9.2% vs 7.9%; EAIR
10.5 vs 9.5. The most common severe AEs during the 52-week period by SOC were infections
and infestations, with no particular PT predominating. Similar overall results were observed in
the long-term extension data (Table 55), with a higher proportion of severe AEs in the
anifrolumab arm as compared to placebo (15.3% vs 9.3%), but an equivalent exposure-adjusted
incidence rate in both arms (EAIR 6.8 for anifrolumab 300mg and EAIR 6.7 for placebo).
Infections and infestations remained the most common severe SOC with PTs of pneumonia and
herpes zoster being the most common: Anifrolumab 300 mg group had 6 cases of severe
pneumonia and 4 cases of severe herpes zoster as compared to 3 cases of severe pneumonia
and 0 cases of severe herpes zoster in the placebo group.

Treatment Emergent Adverse Events and Adverse Reactions
Most subjects experienced a treatment emergent adverse event (TEAE) during the 52-week
period of studies 1013, 04 and 05. Adverse events were reported in 87% of patients receiving
anifrolumab 300 mg and 79% of patients receiving placebo (Source: Table 3.2.1.1.1). Table 56
128
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
lists the frequency of the TEAEs observed during the 52-week period of studies 1013, 04, and 05
by SOC and treatment group. For the most commonly occurring TEAEs ( 5%), the proportion of
subjects who had a TEAE were somewhat higher in the anifrolumab group than the placebo
group for PTs of nasopharyngitis, upper respiratory tract infection, bronchitis, infusion related
reaction, herpes zoster, back pain, and cough (Table 56). Overall, the types and incidences of
common TEAEs were consistent with what would be expected for patients with active SLE who
had been exposed to immunosuppressive therapies and are similar to what has been observed
in other SLE clinical trials.

Laboratory Findings
Hematology
Patients with treatment-emergent changes in clinical hematology and chemistry tests (TELVC)
that fell outside predefined criteria are considered clinically meaningful. The mean hematology
values were generally similar between the anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo groups at baseline
and at Week 52 in the phase 3 studies (data not shown). There were no clinically meaningful
differences noted between the treatment groups in categorical shifts (data not shown). During
the phase 3 52-week study period, there were no notable differences among treatment groups
in the incidence of any potentially clinically important hematology abnormalities based on
prespecified criteria (Table 57). The most frequently observed potentially clinically important
hematology abnormalities were for lymphocytes, with values 0.5 GI/L below extended
reference range observed in 10.4% of patients in the anifrolumab mg group versus 22.4% in the
placebo group. There were also no significant differences in the frequency or pattern of
potentially clinically significant treatment-emergent changes from baseline in hematology
variables during the phase 3 long-term safety data (Table 57). Similar results were seen in the
phase 2 study (1013) 52-week study period (data not shown), including a lower number of
subjects in the anifrolumab 300 mg than placebo group with below normal lymphocyte count at
week 52—5.5% for anifrolumab 300 mg vs 26.8% for placebo.

Clinical Chemistry
In the phase 3 safety pool, no significant differences in mean clinical chemistry values were
noted between anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo and no clinically meaningful trends in change
from baseline values over time were detected (data not shown). Renal function as assessed by
serum creatinine remained stable from baseline to week 52. There were generally no
differences noted between the treatment groups in the frequency and pattern of potentially
clinically significant changes or categorical shifts from baseline in clinical chemistry variables
during the phase 3 studies at 52 weeks or in the phase 3 long-term safety data (Table 58). Two
exceptions were: 1) a slight increase in incidence of elevated gamma glutamyl transferase
during both the 52-week safety period (2.8 % for anifrolumab 300 mg vs 1.1% for placebo) and
phase 3 long-term safety period (3.1% for anifrolumab 300 mg vs 1.4% for placebo: and 2) a
slight increase in incidence of elevated glucose during the phase 3 long-term safety study (7.7%
for anifrolumab 300 mg vs 5.5 % for placebo in phase 3 LTE vs 4.9% and 4.1%, respectively,
during the 52-week study period). 52-week safety data from the phase 2 study (1013) showed
no significant changes in chemistry values that were different between anifrolumab 300 mg and
placebo (data not shown).

a

Patients with baseline laboratory values within extended reference limits and any post-baseline laboratory value
outside extended reference limits (increase and decrease, respectively).
b
Patients with baseline laboratory values within extended reference limits and with any post-baseline laboratory

In the two phase 3 studies, the change from baseline to week 52 in mean specific gravity was
similar in the anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo groups (data not shown). Urine
protein/creatinine ratio (UPCR) values remained stable from baseline to week 52 (data not
shown). There was also no difference noted between the treatment groups in the frequency
and pattern of potentially clinically significant changes in UPCR over 52 weeks of treatment. In
the phase III long-term safety data, the change from baseline to the end of treatment in mean
specific gravity was similar in the anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo groups (data not shown).
From baseline to end of treatment, the change in median UPCR values was -0.12 and 0.46
mg/mmol in the anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo groups, respectively (data not shown). In the
phase III long-term safety data, 2.2% and 2.8% of patients had a UPCR value categorized as a
potentially meaningful increase ( 395 mg/mmol creatinine) in the anifrolumab 300 mg group
and placebo group, respectively (data not shown).
Additional safety data available from study 05 comparing two doses of anifrolumab (150 mg vs
300 mg) may indicate if there were any dose-related patterns for clinically meaningful changes
in hematology and chemistry parameters. There were no notable differences among treatment
groups in the incidence of any potentially clinically important hematology abnormalities based
on prespecified criteria. The most frequently observed potentially clinically important
hematology abnormalities were for lymphocytes, with values 0.5 GI/L below extended
reference range observed in 20.4% and 11.7% of patients in the anifrolumab 150 mg and 300
mg groups, respectively, versus 20.8% in the placebo group (Table 59). These results are similar
to what was observed in the combined phase 3 52-week and long-term safety data (Table 57)
and were not unexpected for this patient population; the majority of these changes were
assessed to be not clinically important. For other hematology parameters (hemoglobin,
hematocrit, leukocytes, monocytes, neutrophils, and platelets), few patients had potentially
clinically important abnormalities.

Vital Signs
During the 52-week study period in the two phase 3 studies, no differences in mean blood
pressure or heart rate were noted between anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo. There were also
no clinically meaningful trends in change from baseline values at 52 weeks (data not shown).
There were also patients with clinically significant treatment-emergent changes in diastolic
blood pressure, systolic blood pressure, or heart rate (data not shown). Similar results were
seen in the phase 2 52-week data (data not shown). In studies 1013, 04 and 05, there were no
clinically important differences among treatment groups in mean changes from baseline in
body weight to week 52.
Electrocardiograms (ECGs)
In all three studies submitted to support approval (1013, 04 and 05), there were no shifts in
ECG parameters from normal to abnormal that were clinically significant and the proportions of
patients with abnormal (not clinically significant) ECG findings were balanced between the
anifrolumab 300 mg group and the placebo group (data not shown). Very few patients in either
treatment group had ECG results fulfilling potentially clinically significant criteria.
QT
QTc data not reported. Data on PR, RR and QRS intervals showed very few subjects with
prolongation or shortened intervals and there were no meaningful differences between
treatment arms (data not shown).
Immunogenicity
In the phase 2 study (1013), 1 patient was positive to anti-drug-antibody (ADA) prior to the first
dose of anifrolumab 300 mg and 5 patients were post dose ADA positive in the anifrolumab 300
mg group. Only one patient had persistent positive ADA for > 16 weeks. Similarly, in study 04,
only one patient had persistent, treatment-induced ADA and in study 05 there were 3 subjects
with persistently positive, treatment-induced ADA. In all phase 3 data, including the long-term
extension study, there were similar proportions of subjects with any ADA or persistent ADA in
both anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo groups, indicating little or no specific induction of ADA
response to anifrolumab. Overall, based on only a limited number of ADA-positive patients,
there was not trend or pattern suggesting an association between the presence of anifrolumab
ADA and the occurrence of AEs, SAEs, DAEs or AESIs. A small number of mild infusion reactions
occurred in both anifrolumab 300 mg and placebo subjects who were ADA positive (data not
shown).

Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues
The following sections address the potential adverse events of special interest (AESIs) specified
in the integrated analysis of safety plan (summarized above in Section 8.2.1, Safety Review
Approach). For ease of review, AESIs will be grouped by Organ System.
Infections
Infections were identified as a topic of interest in the anifrolumab program due to the
immunomodulatory mechanism of action of anifrolumab. Serious non-opportunistic infections,
opportunistic infections, herpes zoster, tuberculosis (TB) including latent TB, and influenza were
analyzed as individual AESI categories. Overall infections were not a category of AESI but are
discussed here as an additional supportive analysis.
Infections overall during treatment, as assessed by the system-organ-class (SOC) Infections and
infestations, were reported in a greater proportion of patients in the anifrolumab 300 mg group
than the placebo group (69.7% vs 57.4%) (Table 61). Most infections in both treatment groups
were mild or moderate in intensity, were not considered serious, and did not result in
discontinuation from treatment. The difference between the overall rates of infection in the
300 mg anifrolumab group versus placebo group was driven by differences in the incidence
rates of mild and moderate infections involving the respiratory tract, excluding pneumonia
(comparable in both treatment groups), and separately, herpes zoster.
The incidence of serious infections during the 52-week treatment periods of the three studies
submitted for approval was similar between the treatment groups: 24/459 patients (5.2%; EAIR
5.4) in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and 32/466 patients (6.9%; EAIR 7.3) in the placebo
group.

driven by differences in the incidence rates of mild and moderate infections involving the

In the phase 3 long-term safety data, including the 120-day safety update through 19 March
2020, the EAIRs of patients with any AE during treatment in the SOC of Infections and
infestations were 107.6/100 PY in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and 91.6/100 PY in the
placebo group. In both treatment groups, among patients with an infection AE, most patients’
events had a maximum reported intensity of mild or moderate. Overall, up to 19 March 2020,
the incidence rates of patients with any infection in the phase III long-term safety data were
consistent with the rates reported in the initial BLA filing.
Non-opportunistic serious infections
During the 52-week study pool (studies 1013, 04 and 05), the proportions of subjects with a
non-opportunistic serious infection were similar in the study arms (Table 62): 4.8% (22/459;
EAIR 5.4) in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and 5.6% (26/466; EAIR 6.6) in the placebo group. In
the placebo group, most non-opportunistic infections were of moderate severity, while in the
anifrolumab 300 mg group, there were a greater number of severe events (11/459 (2.4%) in
anifrolumab 300 mg vs 8/466 ((1.7%) in the placebo group. In the anifrolumab 300 mg group,
events reported in > 2 patients by PT were pneumonia (1.7%, 8/459) and appendicitis (0.7%,
3/459). In the placebo groups, events reported in > 2 patients by PT were pneumonia (1.9%,
9/466) and gastroenteritis (0.6%, 3/466).

Consistent with the 52-week data, the phase 3 long-term safety data demonstrated that patients in

the anifrolumab 300 mg group (9.8 %; EAIR 4.2) had a similar risk for non-opportunistic serious
infections compared with patients in the placebo group (8.2%; EAIR 6.0). In the phase 3 longterm safety data, the most common non-opportunistic serious infection by PT was pneumonia
and similar proportions of patients experienced a serious pneumonia event in the anifrolumab
300 group (2.5%, 9 patients) and the placebo group (2.7%, 10 patients). In the anifrolumab 300
mg group, other non-opportunistic serious infections reported in > 2 patients were: herpes
zoster (1.7%, 6 patients) and pyelonephritis (0.8%, 3 patients). No other non-opportunistic SAE,
by PT, was reported in > 2 patients in either treatment group. In the long-term safety data, nonopportunistic serious infections were reported at rates of 4.2/100 PY in the anifrolumab 300 mg
and 6.0/100 PY in the placebo group, with no trend in either treatment group by yearly
intervals.
Opportunistic infections
During the 52-week study period (studies 1013, 04 and 05), one patient in the anifrolumab 300
mg group had a non-serious opportunistic infection with PT mycobacterium avium complex
infection after 12 doses of anifrolumab that led to discontinuation of IP. The subject was
treated with ethambutol, isoniazid, pyrazinamide, and rifampicin. The event was considered by
the investigator to be moderate in intensity and related to study treatment. Two opportunistic
138
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
infections were reported in the placebo group. One patient had a non-serious AE of
oropharyngeal candidiasis (the same placebo subject also experienced a post-operative wound
infection counted as a treatment emergent serious AESI of non-opportunistic infection). One
patient had a SAE of cryptococcal meningitis with an onset date 32 days after the last dose of IP
(the same placebo subjects also experienced an SLE flare and vasculitis with digital ischemia
that led to discontinuation of IP).
Two additional opportunistic infections occurred during the phase 3 long-term safety period;
one each in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and one in the placebo group. A patient in the
anifrolumab 300 mg group experienced an event of non-serious ophthalmic herpes simplex. A
patient in the placebo group experienced an event of non-serious respiratory moniliasis
(candida albicans).
Herpes Zoster
During the 52-week study period (studies 1013, 04 and 05), patients in the anifrolumab 300 mg
group had an increased risk for herpes zoster infection (Table 63). 28 subjects (6.1%; EAIR 6.9)
experienced a herpes zoster event in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and 6 patients (1.3%; EAIR
1.5) experienced a herpes zoster event in the placebo group.

Of the 28 patients in the anifrolumab 300 mg group with a herpes zoster event during
treatment, 19 patients had cases that were moderate or severe in intensity (Table 63). In the
anifrolumab 300 mg group, 2 patients had a SAE of herpes zoster and 2 patients discontinued IP
due to a herpes zoster AE. In the phase 2 study (1013), one patient in the anifrolumab 300 mg
group experienced SAEs of herpes zoster as well as transverse myelitis with positive CSF PCR for
varicella-zoster virus (study 1013 narratives). In the placebo group, none of the 6 herpes zoster
events were serious or resulted in discontinuation of IP. All herpes zoster cases in both
treatment groups resolved; in the anifrolumab 300 mg group 9 cases resolved with sequelae
(most commonly post-herpetic neuralgia) and in the placebo group 1 case resolved with
sequelae. Most patients received antiviral treatment: all patients in the anifrolumab 300 mg
group and 3 of the 6 patients in the placebo group. Approximately half of the events had an
onset during the first 12 weeks of treatment.
In the phase 3 long-term safety data, a greater proportion of patients in the anifrolumab 300
mg group (42; 11.7%) had a herpes zoster event during treatment than patients in the placebo
group (11; 3.0%) (Table 64). Over up to 4 years of treatment, the EAIR was 5.2/100 PY in the
anifrolumab 300 mg group and 2.2/100 PY in the placebo group, a risk difference of 3.0 (95% CI:
0.9, 5.0) (see Table 3.6.1.1a in Appendix B). Of the 42 events in the anifrolumab 300 mg group,
30 of the events were of moderate to severe intensity. None of the events in the placebo group
were serious or resulted in discontinuation of treatment; in the anifrolumab 300 mg group, 8
were serious and 3 resulted in discontinuation of IP. The EAIR of herpes zoster AEs in the
anifrolumab 300 mg group was highest in Year 1 of treatment (7.3/100 PY) and decreased in
Years 2 through Year 4 (4.1, 3.9, and 2.2 per 100 PY, respectively).

Two of the three studies submitted to support approval (1013 and 05) included doses of
anifrolumab other than 300 mg (see overview discussion in Section 8.2.8 below). For herpes
zoster, specifically, there appears to be a dose-related increased in the frequency and severity
of herpes zoster events. In the phase 3 study 05, a similar frequency AE of herpes zoster
occurred in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm (10/180 subjects; 5.6%) as compared to the
anifrolumab 150 mg arm (5/93 subjects; 5.4%). All 5 events in the 150 mg dose arm were mild
(1) or moderate (4); in the 300 mg dose arm, 2 of the events were mild, 7 were moderate and 1
was severe (disseminated cutaneous, recovered with sequelae of post-herpetic neuralgia). For
the 150 mg dose, herpes zoster events occurred with equal frequency with different study
duration, while half of the events in the 300 mg arm occurred within the first 24 weeks of
treatment (data from study 05 derived from the CSR, Table 11.3.2.2.5). In the phase 2 study
1013, twice as many AE of herpes zoster occurred in the 1000 mg arm (10/105 subjects; 9.5%)
as occurred in the 300 mg arm (5/99 subjects; 5.1%). In the 300 mg arm, herpes zoster events
were labeled as grade 1 (1), grade 2 (3), and grade 3 (1); in the 1000 mg arm, herpes zoster
events were labeled as grade 1 (1), grade 2 (7), and grade 3 (2). In summary, herpes zoster AE
occurred earlier, with greater severity, and with greater frequency in a dose-dependent
fashion.
Tuberculosis (TB) including latent TB
In the anifrolumab clinical program, there were no cases of active TB reported during any study.
To be eligible for the controlled phase II and III studies (studies 1013, 04, and 05), patients were
to have no evidence of active TB or latent TB, unless the latent TB was documented to have
been appropriately treated or active treatment had been initiated (see Section 5.3.1 of the
study 04 and study 05 CSRs and Section 9.3.1 of the study 1013 CSR, in Module 5.3.5.1).
The QFT-G, an interferon gamma release assay (IGRA), is a blood test to aid the diagnosis of
Mycobacterium tuberculosis infection; both latent TB infection and active TB disease. All
patients in the controlled phase II and III studies underwent TB testing at screening and at week
52. If a patient had a positive or indeterminate test during screening, the patient had to meet
additional criteria (e.g., repeat testing, chest x-ray, etc.) prior to the first administration of IP.
In the interim between the screening test and the week 52 test, investigators were asked to
evaluate patients for signs and symptoms of TB at every visit and complete a TB surveillance
questionnaire (studies 04 and 05) or clinical evaluation questionnaire (study 1013) prior to
administration of IP (see sample eCRF in Appendix 12.1.2 to the study 04 CSR in Module 5.3.5.1
and Section 5.2.16 of the study 1013 CSP v3, Appendix 16.1.1 of the CSR in Module 5.3.5.1). In
141
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
the phase III studies, a positive IGRA during the study was recorded as an AESI of latent TB,
provided the patient did not have any signs and symptoms of active TB.
During the 52-week study period (studies 1013, 04 and 05), the number of AEs reported as
latent TB were 6 subjects (1.3%; EAIR 1.3/100 PY) in the anifrolumab 300 mg group (of which 4
occurred in the two phase 3 studies) as compared to 3 subjects (0.6%; EAIR 0.7/100 PY) in the
placebo group (of which 2 occurred in the two phase 3 studies).
During the phase 3 long-term safety study, one additional case of latent TB occurred beyond
the first year of study in the placebo arm for a total of 3 cases over 4 years (EAIR 0.6/100 PY);
14 additional cases of latent TB occurred beyond the first year of study in the anifrolumab 300
mg arm for a total of 18 cases over 4 years (EAIR 2.1/100 PY; risk difference 1.5, 95% CI 0.3,
2.8).
Influenza
During the 52-week study period (studies 1013, 04 and 05), the number of AEs reported as
influenza were 12 subjects (2.6%; EAIR 2.9/100 PY) in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and 9
subjects (1.9%: EAIR 2.3/100 PY) in the placebo group.
During the phase 3 long-term safety study, three additional cases of influenza occurred beyond
the first year of study in the placebo arm for a total of 11 cases over 4 years (EAIR 2.1/100 PY);
14 additional cases of influenza occurred beyond the first year of study in the anifrolumab 300
mg arm for a total of 20 cases over 4 years (EAIR 2.4/100 PY; risk difference 0.2, 95% CI -1.6,
1.8).
Anaphylaxis, hypersensitivity, and infusion-related reactions
In the placebo-controlled phase II and III studies (studies 1013, 04, and 05), patients did not
receive premedication unless they had a prior infusion-related reaction to anifrolumab. If a
prior infusion-related reaction was documented, the investigator could elect to administer
prophylactically an antihistamine and/or acetaminophen/paracetamol for the comfort and
safety of the patient prior to subsequent infusions. Prophylactic use of corticosteroids prior to
subsequent infusions was not permitted.
Anaphylaxis
There were no cases of anaphylaxis in any patient treated with anifrolumab 300 mg in the
primary safety pool, supportive safety pool, or phase III long-term safety data (data not shown).
However, in study 05, one patient in the anifrolumab 150 mg group had an anaphylactic
reaction during the second dose. In the one reported case of anaphylaxis with anifrolumab (b) (6)
patient
in study D3461C00005 - a 27 year old white female from Argentina
experienced a mild pruritic rash after the first dose of study treatment with anifrolumab 150
mg infusion. The patient was prophylactically administered one dose of diphenhydramine prior
142
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
to the second infusion. On the day of Dose 2, during the post-dose flush infusion, the patient
developed anaphylactic reaction with dyspnea, wheezing, flushing, and pruritis; was treated
with hydrocortisone, diphenhydramine, and epinephrine; and was hospitalized for anaphylactic
reaction. Both the initial pruritic rash and anaphylactic reaction were considered to be related
to study treatment with anifrolumab. There were no other anaphylaxis events reported in the
anifrolumab development program.
A possible role for galactose-alpha-1,3-galactose (alpha-gal) glycan 39 contributing to the
potential hypersensitivity and infusion-related reactions was taken into consideration for
(b) (4)
anifrolumab. Glycosylation analysis of anifrolumab showed that it contains
% alpha-gal
on the product Fc domain. It is concluded that alpha-gal glycan in anifrolumab has low risk to
contribute to these events because of the following considerations (provided by the Applicant
in response to an Information Request): 1) anifrolumab does not have any glycosylation sites in
the Fab region, and the -Gal is present in the conserved glycosylation site in the Fc domain,
whereas monoclonal antibodies for which pre-formed anti-IgE contribute to anaphylaxis have
alpha-gal in both the Fab and Fc domains; 2) none of the anaphylaxis/hypersensitivity cases
happened during the 1st infusion, and 3) There was no distinct regional pattern (tick area).
Therefore, testing for alpha-gal IgE in the single anaphylaxis case with 150 mg anifrolumab is
not needed.
Hypersensitivity
During the 52-week study period (studies 1013, 04 and 05), there were more AEs categorized as
hypersensitivity reactions in the anifrolumab 300 mg group than the placebo group: 13 patients
(2.8%) and 3 patients (0.6%), respectively (Table 65). In the primary safety pool, all
hypersensitivity AEs were mild or moderate in intensity, and all events in the anifrolumab 300
mg group resolved spontaneously or with symptomatic treatment. One patient in the
anifrolumab 300 mg group had a hypersensitivity SAE, was treated, and continued with the IP
dosing schedule with prophylaxis given for subsequent infusions (data not shown). One patient
in the placebo group had a hypersensitivity AE that led to discontinuation of IP. During the 52week treatment period, all hypersensitivity AEs occurred in the first 24 weeks of treatment
(data not shown).

Infusion-related reactions
During the 52-week study period (studies 1013, 04 and 05), there were more AEs categorized as
infusion-related reactions in the anifrolumab 300 mg group than the placebo group: 43 patients
(9.4%) and 33 patients (7.1%), respectively (Table 66). All infusion-related reaction AEs were
mild or moderate in intensity, and there were no serious infusion-related reactions. One patient
in the placebo group had an infusion-related reaction that led to discontinuation of IP. During
the 52-week treatment period, most infusion-related reaction AEs occurred in the first 24
weeks of treatment (data not shown).

Data on specific infusion related reaction (IRR) symptoms is provided for the two phase 3
studies, in which a total number of 79 IRR events occurred in the anifrolumab 300 mg group (39
subjects) as compared to 59 IRR events in the placebo group (26 subjects). Among the events in
the anifrolumab 300 mg group, the most common symptoms (reported in 5% of events) were
headache (45.6% of events), nausea (22.8% of events), vomiting (7.6% of events), and fatigue
(5.1% of events) (data not shown).
During the phase 3 long-term safety study, 3 additional cases of IRR occurred beyond the first
year of study in the placebo arm for a total of 30 cases over 4 years (EAIR 6.3/100 PY); 11
additional cases of IRR occurred beyond the first year of study in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm
for a total of 52 cases over 4 years (EAIR 1.9/100 PY).
Malignancies
According to the Summary of Clinical Safety, malignancies were identified in the anifrolumab
clinical studies based on the MedDRA SMQs listed in Section 1.1.4.3. Across the phase II and III
SLE controlled and uncontrolled studies of IV anifrolumab (studies 02, 04, 05, 09, 1013, and
1145), there were 14 malignancies reported in patients who received any dose of anifrolumab
(see Listing 1, Appendix 2.7.4.7.3 in Module 5.3.5.3). Only non-melanoma skin cancers (n = 7)
and breast cancers (n = 3) were reported in more than 1 patient. Among 6 total malignancies
reported in anifrolumab-treated patients in the 52-week phase studies, there were 4
malignancies reported within 180 days of the first exposure to anifrolumab, which may have
been pre-existing: 2 cases of squamous cell carcinoma in the anifrolumab 300 mg group (31
days and 51 days after the patient’s first dose, respectively), an invasive breast carcinoma in the
anifrolumab 150 mg group (113 days after the first dose), and a B-cell lymphoma in the
anifrolumab 300 mg group (12 days after the first dose) (data not shown).
During the 52-week study period (studies 1013, 04 and 05), there were a total of 6 subjects
(1.3%; EAIR 1.3) with any malignancy in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and 3 subjects (0.6%;
EAIR 0.7) with any malignancy in the placebo group (Table 67). The risk difference between the
anifrolumab 300 mg group and the placebo group was 0.6 (95% CI -0.8, 2.2).

A malignancy during study is defined as a malignancy with a date of onset day of first dose of IP and

During the phase 3 long-term safety study, 2 additional cases of malignancy occurred beyond

with long-term exposure to anifrolumab, with no temporal patterns observed by yearly
intervals of malignancies during treatment (data not shown).
MACE
Major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE) events were identified in the two phase 3 studies
(04 and 05) by an independent committee adjudicating cardiovascular events. As the phase 2
study (1013) did not include an independent cardiovascular adjudication committee, the events
included in the pool of all three 52-week studies were identified by a custom MedDRA term list.
Only four MACE events were recorded. One patient in the anifrolumab 300 mg group had an AE
of acute coronary syndrome (study 04). Three patients with MACE events occurred in the
placebo group of study 1013—two of the patients had an ischemic stroke and one had a
cerebral infarction.
Vasculitis (non-SLE)
Vasculitis has been evaluated because of findings of focal arteritis in a non-clinical study.
However, non-SLE vasculitis was not observed in any anifrolumab-treated patient in any of the
integrated safety pools.
