Clinical Outcome Assessment (COA) Analyses Informing
Safety/Tolerability
N/A
Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups
Study 1013 was analyzed for AE by subgroup separately from the two phase 3 studies and is
included in the study 1013 CSR. Of the subjects who were 45 years old at baseline, the
proportion who had at least one TEAE was 85.3% (116/136) in the total anifrolumab group and
76.1% (54/71) in the placebo group. Similarly, of the subjects who were > 45 years old at
baseline, the proportion who had at least one TEAE was 85.3% (58/68) in the total anifrolumab
group and 80.0% (24/30) in the placebo group (Section 14, Table 14.3.1.31). Of the female
subjects, the proportion who had at least one TEAE was 84.5% (163/193) in the total
anifrolumab group and 78.3% (72/92) in the placebo group. The number of male subjects who
had at least one TEAE at baseline was too low to analyze (Section 14, Table 14.3.1.33).
Data pooled from the two phase 3 study 52-week studies is presented for subgroup analysis.
For baseline demographics, refer to Table 12. Overall, the AE profile was consistent across all
pre-defined subgroups, including gender, age (< or 65 years old), race, underlying renal or
hepatic function, BMI at baseline (< or 28 kg/m2), baseline disease-related factors (SLEDAI-2K
< or 10 points; IFN gene signature high or low), and based on anti-drug antibody (ADA) status.
Additional analysis for the effect of geographic region, and subgroups based on concomitant
medication use at baseline is also presented.
1. Gender
The majority (>90%)of subjects were female. Although based on a limited sample size of
males, the safety profile of anifrolumab was generally similar between men and women
with no clinically meaningful differences observed in the frequency or pattern of AEs.
2. Age
The proportion of patients in the program over the age of 65 was small (3.2%). Based on
limited data, the safety profile appears to be similar in patients 65 years compared to
those < 65 years of age. No clinically meaningful differences were observed in the frequency
or pattern of AEs. Anifrolumab has not been studied in pediatric subjects < 18 years of age.
3. Race
Although the primary safety pool study population was predominantly White (65.9% of
patients), the AE profile of anifrolumab was generally similar across racial groups for up to
52 weeks of treatment. No clinically meaningful differences were observed in the
frequencies or patterns of AEs.
4. Underlying renal and hepatic function
147
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
Patients with mild and moderate renal impairment were enrolled, but the phase 3 studies
did not include patients with severe renal impairment. Most patients had normal kidney
function (eGFR 90 mL/min/1.73 m2)—65.8% and 67.9% in the anifrolumab 300 mg group
and placebo group, respectively. About one third of patients in each group had eGFR values
characterized as mild disease (60 to 89 mL/min/1.73 m2). Few ( 4% in each group) had
eGFR values categorized as moderate disease (30 to 59 ml/min/1.73m2).
Anifrolumab clearance is target-mediated and the primary clearance mechanism is neither
hepatic nor renal; it binds to the IFNAR receptors, is internalized and subsequently cleared.
Based on the population PK analysis, the clearance of anifrolumab in patients with mild,
moderate decrease in eGFR was comparable to those with normal eGFR values (see Section
6.9.2.4 of the Population PK Report in Module 5.3.5.3). Similarly, based on the population
PK analysis, baseline hepatic function tests (AST, ALT, and total bilirubin) had no clinically
relevant impact on the clearance of anifrolumab (see Section 6.9.2.5 of the Population PK
Report in Module 5.3.5.3).
5. Body mass index
The safety profile by BMI at baseline (< 28, 28 kg/m2) is summarized for the primary safety
pool in data displays of AEs by category in Tables 3.8.7.1 and 3.8.7.2, Appendix 2.7.4.7.1 in
Module 5.3.5.3. Summaries of most common AEs and SAEs by BMI are presented in Tables
3.8.18, 3.8.27.1, and 3.8.27.2, Appendix 2.7.4.7.1 in Module 5.3.5.3. The safety profile of
anifrolumab is generally similar in patients with BMI 28 kg/m2 compared with those < 28
kg/m2 for up to 52 weeks of treatment. No clinically meaningful differences were observed
in the frequency or pattern of AEs.
6. Baseline disease-related factors (SLEDAI-2K and IFN gene signature)
In the phase 3 52-week safety pool, 71.6% of patients had baseline SLEDAI-2K 10 points.
The safety profile of anifrolumab is generally similar in patients with SLEDAI-2K 10 points
compared with those < 10 points for up to 52 weeks of treatment. No clinically meaningful
differences were observed in the frequency or pattern of AEs.
Approximately 82.6% of patients in the phase 3 52-week safety pool were IFN gene
signature high at screening. The safety profile of anifrolumab is generally similar in patients
with IFN gene signature high compared with low for up to 52 weeks of treatment. No
clinically meaningful differences were observed in the frequency or pattern of AEs.
7. ADA status
There was no apparent trend or pattern suggesting a correlation between the presence of
anifrolumab ADA and occurrence of AEs, SAEs, DAEs, or AESIs, including hypersensitivity or
anaphylaxis events. However, given that few patients were positive for ADA in the phase 3
52-week safety pool (25 patients in the anifrolumab 300 mg group and 35 patients in the
placebo group), no definitive conclusions can be drawn.

148
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
8. Geographic region
The safety profile by geographic region (Asia Pacific/Europe/Latin America/North America)
in the primary safety pool is summarized in data displays of AEs by category in Tables
3.8.9.1 and 3.8.9.2, Appendix 2.7.4 in Module 5.3.5.3. The safety profile of anifrolumab was
generally similar in patients across geographic regions in the phase 3 52-week data. No
clinically meaningful differences were observed in the frequency or pattern of AEs.
9. Baseline concomitant medications—steroids and immunosuppressant therapy
In the phase 3 52-week safety pool, 51.7% of patients were taking a daily dose of oral
corticosteroids (OCS) of 10 mg/day, and 48.1% of patients were on other
immunosuppressant therapy at baseline. The overall safety profile of anifrolumab was
generally similar in subjects across baseline OCS dose and in patients taking or not taking
immunosuppressant therapy at baseline.
General infection risk in individuals with SLE is higher in subjects on steroids and
immunosuppressant therapy. 40 Subjects with underlying autoimmune disease may be at
increased risk for infections. As an example, herpes zoster is more common in individuals
with rheumatoid arthritis and SLE. 41 In SLE patients, the risk of herpes zoster is higher in
patients being treated with steroids and immunosuppressants. 42 As the anifrolumab 300 mg
group had a significantly higher rate of herpes zoster as compared to the placebo group, it
would be helpful to know if individuals in either group who developed herpes zoster were
also taking either steroids 10 mg/day or other immunosuppressant therapy. This may
provide information regarding a possible additive effect (increased risk) of herpes zoster in
subjects prescribed anifrolumab who are also taking OCS or immunosuppressants. The
overall risk for herpes zoster for the phase 3 52-week safety data is shown with additional
data according to baseline OCS use in Table 68 and with additional data according to
baseline immunosuppressant use in Table 69.

For OCS use, there does not appear to be an increased (additive) risk of herpes zoster since the
number of subjects who developed herpes zoster is similar between low and high dose OCS for
both the anifrolumab (11 vs 12) and the placebo group (2 vs 3). However, for concomitant
immunosuppressant use, there were a higher number of subjects taking any
immunosuppressant who developed herpes zoster than subjects not on any
immunosuppressant in the anifrolumab group (17 vs 6). This suggests that there may be an
increased (additive) risk of herpes zoster in SLE subjects who are treated with both anifrolumab
and immunosuppressant therapy. A similar conclusion has been recently presented in a
published summary of the safety profile of anifrolumab--the authors found that subgroup
analysis of pooled data from studies 04 and 05 did not suggest any clear trends in herpes zoster
cases based on demographics, baseline disease characteristics or concomitant medication use.
However, a greater number of herpes zoster events occurred in patients receiving anifrolumab
together with concomitant immunosuppressants (9.8%, n=17) than in those not receiving
immunosuppressants (3.2%, n=6); the risk difference for subjects on immunosuppressants is

reported as 8.1 (95% CI 2.6, 13.7) comparing those treated with anifrolumab (9.8%, n=17) to
those on placebo (1.7%, n=3). 43
For comparison, there appears to be an increased (additive) risk for serious pneumonia when
combining anifrolumab with higher dose steroids (5 vs 1) but not for concomitant
immunosuppressant use (3 vs 3). These observations are based on small subgroups of patients
in the phase 3 studies and should be interpreted with caution. Additional safety data is required
to determine how concomitant use of higher dose steroids and other immunosuppressant
therapy may increase the risk of herpes zoster and other serious infections in SLE subjects
treated with anifrolumab.
Specific Safety Studies/Clinical Trials
N/A
Additional Safety Explorations
Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development
Due to the limited species specificity of anifrolumab, there are no animal models to evaluate
the quantitative or dose-dependent risk of carcinogenicity associated with anifrolumab
administration. The direct carcinogenic risks associated with anifrolumab administration are not
known. Administration of murine IFN- / enhanced antitumor effects in mice that contained
transplanted tumors. In IFNAR1- and IFNAR2-deficient mice or mice treated with analogous
murine anti- IFNAR1 or IFN- / antibodies, decreased host defense to tumors was observed.
The relevance of these experimental findings in mouse models for malignancy risk in humans is
unknown.
Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
Overall, in the anifrolumab SLE clinical program there were 31 patients with one or more
pregnancy as of 01 August 2019. Of those 31 patients, 20 patients were randomized to receive
anifrolumab. Among the patients who received anifrolumab and reported a pregnancy, no
congenital anomalies associated with IP or no drug-associated AEs were observed.
Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth
Pediatric studies were deferred until after completion of phase 3 studies in adults.

Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound
Doses of up to 1000 mg IV in clinical studies in patients with SLE are associated with an
acceptable safety profile. There was no event of overdose reported during clinical development
with anifrolumab and, when administered IV, there is low potential for overdose. There is no
specific treatment for an overdose with anifrolumab. If overdose occurs, the patient should be
treated supportively with appropriate monitoring and symptomatic treatment as necessary.
There is no evidence for and no anticipation of patient abuse or dependence potential with
anifrolumab.
In study 1013, all patients were to be followed from the last dose of IP (week 48) through the
end of the safety follow-up period (week 60). An analysis of those patients did not identify any
adverse effects of drug withdrawal after stopping anifrolumab treatment (see study 1013 CSR
addendum in Module 5.3.5.1).
Suicidality and depression
During phase 3 studies 04 and 05 (52-week treatment period), The eight-item Patient Health
Questionnaire depression scale (PHQ-8) scores were similar across all treatment groups at
baseline with no clinically meaningful changes from baseline to week 52; small and similar
decreases in PHQ-8 scores were observed in all treatment arms from baseline to week 52.
There were few AEs related to depression as assessed by the MedDRA SMQ depression
(excluding suicide and self-injury) with balanced events between the treatment groups (Table
70). Few patients had AEs related to suicidality; no patients in the anifrolumab 300 mg arms; 2
patients in placebo (PTs: suicide attempt and suicidal ideation). There was no evidence of
increased risk in suicidal ideation or behavior based on Columbia-Suicide Severity Rating Scale
(C-SSRS)—5 subjects in the anifrolumab arms vs 10 in placebo arms. Depression (PT) in
anifrolumab 300 mg vs placebo was 18 (5.0%)/EAIR 2.0 vs 13 (3.6%) EAIR 2.3.

Integrated Assessment of Safety
Overall, the safety profile of anifrolumab IV assessed at a dose of 300 mg IV every 4 weeks in
the adult SLE population that participated in the three studies submitted to support approval
(1013, 04 and 05) appeared to be similar to placebo, with the exception of certain infections
and infusion related reactions.
There were only 2 deaths in the anifrolumab 300 mg IV group during the 52-week study period.
No patients randomized to the placebo plus standard of care group died during the 52-week
study period. In the entire anifrolumab IV clinical development program, including doses of 150
mg and 1000 mg IV during a 52-week period and ongoing, long-term, phase 3 extension study
of 300 mg (up to a total of 3 years), there were 11 deaths in the anifrolumab IV group and 3
deaths in the placebo group. In long-term, placebo controlled assessment of subjects on
anifrolumab 300 mg, a total of seven deaths were attributed to infections (4 pneumonia and 3
COVID-19) in anifrolumab-treated subjects as compared to none on placebo. Deaths in the
anifrolumab group were more closely associated with infections, particularly pneumonias.
There were only 6 malignancies in the anifrolumab 300 mg IV group during the 52-week study
period and 3 malignancies in the placebo group. Adding data from the ongoing phase 3 longterm extension study of 300 mg, there were 2 additional malignancies in the placebo group for
a total of 5, as compared to 6 in the anifrolumab 300 mg IV group.
Many of the serious adverse events and withdrawals from study treatment in the anifrolumab
IV safety database were related to underlying disease activity or infections. The number of
subjects with any infection was higher in the anifrolumab IV group (69.7%) than the placebo
group (55.4%) with increased rates of Nasopharyngitis (anifrolumab 300 16.3% vs placebo
9.4%); Upper Respiratory Infection (15.5% vs 9.7%); Bronchitis (9.8% vs 4.3%); Herpes Zoster
(6.1% vs 1.3%); Influenza (2.6% vs 1.9%); and Latent TB (0.9% vs (0.2%).
Herpes zoster infections occurred in a higher proportion (6.9%) of anifrolumab IV patients than
placebo (1.9%) during the 52-week study period.
There were no cases of active tuberculosis. An increased rate of latent TB occurred in the
anifrolumab arms, in particular during the long-term extension study. The relationship between
153
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
tuberculosis and SLE is unclear, although a high suspicion of TB and planned monitoring for
active and latent TB should be considered, in particular in SLE patients from endemic countries
and with high cumulative steroid exposure. 44
MACE events were rare and balanced between anifrolumab and placebo arms.
There were no cases of suicide/self-injury.
There was one case of anaphylaxis with the anifrolumab 150 mg IV dose and no cases of
anaphylaxis with the anifrolumab 300 mg IV dose. There were more infusion related reactions
(9.4% vs 7.1%) and hypersensitivity reactions (2.6% vs 0.6%) in the anifrolumab 300 mg IV
group, including two cases of angioedema, as compared to the placebo group.
Limitations associated with the anifrolumab IV safety database include the lack of concomitant
IV cyclophosphamide or other biologics including those that target B cells (e.g., rituximab or
belimumab), and the lack of patients with severe renal lupus and central nervous system
disease. The small numbers of patients available for subgroup analysis of gender (males), age (
65 years of age as well as children 18 years of age), and race precludes determination of the
product’s safety profile in these subgroups.
As reviewed above, there may be an increased (additive) risk for herpes zoster with the
concomitant use of immunosuppressive therapies and anifrolumab. There may also be an
increased risk for pneumonia with the concomitant use of steroids and anifrolumab. The data
suggests an increased risk of potentially fatal infections associated with anifrolumab use. There
is a possible increased risk of nervous system disorders that requires further assessment.
Similarly, additional long-term data is required to determine possible associations between
anifrolumab and chemistry abnormalities such as increased serum glucose and gamma glutamyl
transferase.
In conclusion, the overall safety profile of anifrolumab is acceptable given the adequate
demonstration of benefit in SLE patients.

Statistical Issues
There were two primary statistical issues that arose during this review; missing data and the
question of whether the submission provided substantial evidence of efficacy.
The amount of missing data for the three studies was moderate to high. It was pre-specified

44

Balbi GGM, Machado-Ribeiro F, Marques CDL, Signorelli F, Levy RA. The Interplay Between Tuberculosis and
Systemic Lupus Erythematosus. Curr Opin Rheumatol 2018 Jul;30(4):395-402.

154
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
that missing week 52 data would be imputed by available week 48 data. Considering these
patients as missing, in study 1013, there was approximately 14% missing data at week 24, and
23% at week 52. Similarly, study 05 and 04 had roughly 23% missing data at week 52.
Use of such a last observation carried forward (LOCF) approach is generally not recommended
as the underlying missingness mechanisms are generally not scientifically justified. In particular,
LOCF imputation makes the strong assumption that the outcome of a participant does not
change after drop out 45. Additionally, LOCF is a single imputation approach, which generally
results in underestimation of the uncertainty of the estimated treatment effect due to the
failure to specifically account for the lack of knowledge of the values that are missing. The
approach to handling missing data should (1) properly capture the statistical uncertainty in
parameter estimation due to data missingness; and (2) reflect what is considered to be most
clinically plausible.
In order to evaluate the potential impact of all missing data, including those subjects whose
data would be imputed based on available week 48 data, we evaluated the potential impact of
these patients having different outcomes on the primary endpoint for study 1013 and 04, and
additionally on BICLA at week 52 for study 1013 and study 05. These sensitivity analyses
showed that the missing data were relatively unlikely to impact the results and conclusions. The
results are further supported by the consistent treatment effects in BICLA at week 52 across
studies.
The Draft Guidance for Industry: Demonstrating Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness for
Human Drug and Biological Products 46 describes two possible approaches to demonstrating
substantial evidence: (1) two adequate and well-controlled trials or (2) one adequate and wellcontrolled trial plus confirmatory evidence. Study 04 represents an adequate and wellcontrolled trial as a large, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, phase 3
study which was successful on its primary and key secondary endpoints. Though the primary
endpoint was changed after the results of study 05 were made known, this change was made
before study unblinding. After a review of the DSMB meeting minutes, there were no concerns
over the integrity of the trial or the impact on the estimated treatment effect due to the change
to the primary endpoint.
Study 1013 was a dose-ranging, phase 2, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebocontrolled trial. The primary endpoint of SRI-4 + OCS tapering at week 24 was significant using
the pre-specified alpha=0.10. However, the study did not specify an appropriate multiplicity
procedure for the testing of multiple populations and multiple doses, and therefore study 1013
is not considered to be an adequate and well-controlled trial. Nevertheless, the results of BICLA
and SRI-4 at week 52, and other exploratory secondary endpoints were nominally significant

45

National Research Council. The prevention and treatment of missing data in clinical trials. National Academies
Press, 2010.
46
https://www.fda.gov/media/133660/download

155
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
and notably consistent with the results of study 04, providing evidence that anifrolumab is
effective.
Additional support for effectiveness comes from study 05. Although study 05 failed on the
primary endpoint of SRI-4 at week 52 and as such, efficacy results are considered exploratory,
the results of BICLA at week 52, both under the original protocol-specified rules and under the
post-hoc set of restricted medication rules as defined in study 04, demonstrated a similar
treatment effect of anifrolumab 300mg to the treatment effect in study 04.
Results on secondary endpoints, though considered exploratory, were also consistent with
results observed in study 04 and study 1013. Thus, while there are limitations to the evidence
provided by a study which failed to win on its primary endpoint, when considering these results
in combination with those observed in study 04 and study 1013, we consider this further
evidence of effectiveness.

Conclusions and Recommendations
To support the proposed indication, the Applicant provided the results from three trials, that
evaluated anifrolumab IV 300 mg every 4 weeks as well as two additional doses (150 mg every
4 weeks and 1000 mg every 4 weeks) versus placebo in a total of 1,123 adults with active
systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) on stable immunosuppressive background medications.
These were international, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, 52-week
trials that evaluated the efficacy, safety, and immunogenicity of anifrolumab 300 mg IV every 4
weeks versus placebo in 925 adult subjects with active SLE receiving standard therapy.
Study 04 achieved statistically significant and clinically meaningful results using primary
endpoints incorporating either SRI-4 or BICLA composite endpoints at week 52. Study 1013
demonstrated nominally significant results in favor of anifrolumab on BICLA and SRI-4 at week
52, and in study 05, there was an observed improved clinical outcome in anifrolumab compared
to placebo in the pre-specified and post-hoc analysis for BICLA at week 52. In study 04, a higher
proportion of anifrolumab IV subjects (47.8%) achieved the 52-week BICLA primary endpoint as
compared to the placebo group (31.3%)[risk difference (95% CI): 16.3 (6.3, 26.3)]. In study 1013,
a higher proportion of anifrolumab IV subjects (53.5%) achieved the 52-week BICLA secondary
endpoint as compared to the placebo group (25.7%)[risk difference (95% CI): 28.4 (15.3, 41.5)].
In study 05 post-hoc analysis, a higher proportion of anifrolumab IV subjects (47.2%) achieved
the 52-week BICLA endpoint as compared to the placebo group (29.9%)[risk difference (95% CI):
17.0 (7.2, 26.8)]; however, for its primary 52-week SRI-4 endpoint, study 05 did not
demonstrate statistically significant superiority of anifrolumab IV over placebo. Additional
supportive evidence for efficacy is provided by the SRI-4 composite 52-week endpoint achieved
in study 04 by a higher proportion of anifrolumab IV subjects (55.6%) as compared to the
placebo group (37.4%)[risk difference (95% CI):18.2 (8.1, 28.3)] and in study 1013 by a higher
proportion of anifrolumab IV subjects (62.6%) as compared to the placebo group (40.2%)[risk
156
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
difference (95% CI):24.0 (10.9, 37.2)]. The proposed dose of 300 mg IV every 4 weeks
demonstrated consistent benefit both for primary BICLA and SRI-4 endpoints as well as for
secondary endpoints of maintaining steroid tapering and reduction in rate of SLE flare. There
was also a qualitative improvement with 300 mg anifrolumab vs placebo for reduction in
inflammatory joint symptoms and SLE skin disease severity.
Based on the above considerations, study 04 is considered an adequate and well controlled trial
and provides the primary basis of substantial evidence of effectiveness of anifrolumab. The
study showed favorable and statistically significant changes on the pre-specified, clinically
meaningful primary and secondary endpoints when comparing anifrolumab 300 mg IV versus
placebo. Specifically, a favorable and statistically significant BICLA response rate compared to
placebo at week 52 was achieved with anifrolumab 300 mg IV.
These results were consistent with those from study 1013; however, study 1013 is not
considered an adequate and well controlled since an appropriate method for controlling
multiplicity was not pre-specified. While study 05 did not show statistically significant
improvement in the prespecified primary endpoint (SRI-4), study 05 did show nominally
significant changes in the key secondary BICLA endpoint in favor of anifrolumab 300 mg IV
dose. Also, disease activity measured by the SLE disease activity index (updated in 2000),
(SLEDAI-2K), showed a dose-dependent separation in favor of anifrolumab in study 05. In all
three trials, additional confirmatory evidence was noted in the analyses of the secondary
endpoints, including steroid-use reduction, and improvement in mucocutaneous disease
activity. There was also a reduction in annualized flare rate in study 04 and a reduction in
arthritis in studies 1013 and 05 when anifrolumab 300 mg IV was compared to placebo,
providing additional supportive evidence of efficacy.
The safety of anifrolumab in the SLE clinical program was consistent with expected increased
risk of infection due to immunosuppression, in particular for viral infections in which Type I IFN
is a central part of the immune response. While the 52-week incidence of serious infections
between placebo and anifrolumab arms in all three trials submitted to support approval was
balanced between anifrolumab and placebo arms—anifrolumab 4.8% (22/459) vs placebo 5.6%
(26/466)—the proportion of subjects with any infections was higher in subjects treated with
anifrolumab 300 mg (69.7%; 320/459) as compared to subjects in the placebo arms (55.4%;
EAIR 99.9/100 PY). Herpes zoster infections, in particular, occurred at a higher rate in
anifrolumab-treated subjects compared to placebo (6.1% vs 1.3%). In the 52-week controlled
study period, fatal infections occurred in 0.4% of patients receiving anifrolumab and 0.2% of
patients receiving placebo. In long-term, placebo controlled assessment of subjects on
anifrolumab 300 mg beyond 52 weeks, a total of seven deaths were attributed to infections in
anifrolumab-treated subjects (four pneumonia and three COVID-19) as compared to none on
placebo. Certain infections occurred more frequently in patients treated with anifrolumab,
including Nasopharyngitis (anifrolumab 300 16.3% vs placebo 9.4%); Upper Respiratory
Infection (15.5% vs 9.7%); Bronchitis (9.8% vs 4.3%); Herpes Zoster (6.1% vs 1.3%); Influenza
(2.6% vs 1.9%); and Latent TB (0.9% vs (0.2%).
157
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
There was one case of anaphylaxis with the anifrolumab 150 mg IV dose and no cases of
anaphylaxis with the anifrolumab 300 mg or 1000 mg IV doses. There were more infusion
related reactions (9.4% vs 7.1%) and hypersensitivity reactions (2.6% vs 0.6%), including 2 cases
of angioedema, in the anifrolumab 300 mg IV group as compared to the placebo group.
In summary, the increased risk for infections, in particular respiratory infections and herpes
zoster, the increased risk for potentially fatal outcome from serious infections, and increased
risk of hypersensitivity and infusion-related reactions for anifrolumab 300 mg is similar to the
only other approved targeted therapy for SLE and represents an acceptable safety profile for
this indication.
Lower SLE disease activity as well as a decrease in the risk for severe SLE flares and a reduction
in daily steroid dose may result in less long-term end-organ damage from patients’ underlying
disease, less treatment with immunosuppressive agents commonly used to treat SLE with their
own inherent toxicities, as well as fewer hospitalizations with less loss of absent work days and
ultimately less morbidity and mortality associated with this disease.
In summary, the efficacy and safety data generated from one adequate and well-controlled
study—04—as well as confirmatory evidence from studies 1013 and 05, result in a favorable
benefit-risk assessment for approval of the 300 mg dosing regimen of anifrolumab IV as add-on
treatment for adult patients with active SLE while receiving standard therapy consisting of
either one or any combination of oral corticosteroids (OCS), antimalarials and/or
immunosuppressants.
If approved, anifrolumab, a first-in-class biologic with a unique mechanism of action, will
provide a new treatment option for patients with SLE.

1) The tradename SAPHNELO® is acceptable. It has been deemed acceptable by both
DMETS and the Division.
2) The indication under Section 1 Indications and Usage is the following: “SAPHNELO
(anifrolumab-fnia) is indicated for the treatment of adult patients with moderate to
severe systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE), who are receiving standard therapy [see

to

is a type I interferon (IFN) receptor antagonist.” Anifrolumab is not included in the FDA

recommended EPC term for SAPHNELO based on its mechanism of action.

Precautions (5.3)].

13. Postmarketing Requirements and Commitment
The review team considered the following issues to determine which post-marketing
requirements should be addressed by the Applicant:
1. Significant proportion of patients who may receive anifrolumab are women of childbearing
potential and it is expected that pregnancies may occur in women who use Saphnelo. The
pre-approval safety database for Saphnelo was too limited to draw any conclusions about
the effect of Saphnelo on obstetrical, maternal and fetal/neonatal outcomes. FDA requests
an observational prospective pregnancy registry to examine the pregnancy and birth
outcomes with Saphnelo use. The goal of this PMR registry study is to evaluate the effects
of Saphnelo on obstetrical, maternal and fetal/neonatal outcomes. This is a registry-based,
prospective observational exposure cohort study and will compare Saphnelo-exposed
pregnancy to a disease-matched Saphnelo unexposed pregnancy cohort. Pregnant women
18 years of age at the time of enrollment, with or without Saphnelo exposure will be
enrolled. The registry will identify and record major and minor congenital malformations,
spontaneous abortions, stillbirths, elective terminations, small for gestational age, and any
other adverse pregnancy outcomes. We request a prospective patient registry,
complimentary to an electronic claims-based retrospective cohort study. The pregnancy
registry ensures that exposed pregnant women are identified with data collection on
baseline risk factors and safety outcomes prospectively identified with medical chart
adjudication.
2. It is expected that pregnancies in women who use Saphnelo, target premenopausal women.
The pre-approval safety database for Saphnelo was too limited to draw any conclusions
about the effect of Saphnelo on pregnancies, maternal and fetal/neonatal outcomes. The
goal of this observational study is to evaluate the effects of Saphnelo on obstetrical,
maternal and fetal/neonatal outcomes. This study will use claims or electronic medical
record data to assess major congenital malformations, early pregnancy loss, placenta
previa, preeclampsia, premature delivery, and small for gestational age in women expose to
Saphnelo during pregnancy compared to an unexposed pregnancy control population. The
observational study will assess the obstetrical, maternal, fetal and neonatal outcomes in
Saphnelo-exposed pregnancies compared to an unexposed pregnant control population.
This study will complement the prospective pregnancy registry study.
3. There are no data on the presence of anifrolumab in human milk, the effects on the
breastfed infant, or the effects on milk production. The lack of clinical data during lactation
precludes a clear determination of the risk of anifrolumab to an infant during lactation. The
Division of Pediatric and Maternal Health determined that a PMR for a milk only lactation
study is in order because the drug is an NME and use of Saphnelo (anifrolumab) is
anticipated in females of reproductive potential; therefore, additional data are required to
evaluate the quantity of drug, if any, that is transferred into human breastmilk.
4. See section 10 for explanation of PREA PMR pediatric trial.

168
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE

Thus, the post-marketing requirements agreed on with the Applicant include:
1. Conduct a prospective pregnancy registry to evaluate the effects of Saphnelo
(anifrolumab-fnia) on pregnancy and maternal and fetal/neonatal outcomes. This
pregnancy registry study may be conducted as part of a multiple-product or diseasebased pregnancy registry.
2. Conduct a retrospective cohort study in a claims-based database to evaluate the effects
of Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) on pregnancy-related outcomes.
3. Perform a lactation study, milk only, in lactating women who have received Saphnelo
(anifrolumab-fnia) to assess concentrations of anifrolumab-fnia in breast milk using a
validated assay. A mother-infant pair study may be required in the future depending on
the results of this milk-only study.
4. Conduct a study to evaluate the safety, efficacy, and pharmacokinetics of anifrolumabfnia plus background standard therapy in pediatric subjects ages 5 years to 17 years of
age with active systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE).
There is a postmarketing commitment not subject to the reporting requirements under section
(b) (4)

14. Division Director (Clinical) Comments
The Applicant, Astra Zeneca, has submitted adequate data to support approval of anifrolumab,
a type I interferon (IFN) receptor antagonist monoclonal antibody, 300 mg/2 mL (150 mg/mL) in
a single-dose vial for intravenous infusion over a 30 minute period every 4 weeks, for the
treatment of adult patients with moderate to severe systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE), who
are receiving standard therapy. SLE is a serious and potentially life-threatening systemic
autoimmune disease with significant morbidity and mortality as well as impact patients’
function and quality of life. The toxicities associated with both the approved and the off-label
SLE treatments contribute to the long-term morbidity and mortality observed in patients with
SLE, highlighting the high unmet medical need in this disease.
The application raised important regulatory and scientific questions that were adequately
addressed by the review team, as detailed in this document, which I will only summarize in my
comments. Specifically, after the successful proof of concept, dose ranging phase 2 study
(1013), the first of the phase 3 studies (05) did not meet its primary endpoint, SRI-4 at week 52.
However, based on analyses of data from these two studies, the Applicant proposed a revised
primary endpoint from SRI-4 to BICLA at week 52, of the second still blinded phase 3 study (04).
Study 04 met its primary endpoint of BICLA at week 52, along with statistically positive results
on the SRI-4 endpoint, ranked now a key secondary endpoint. Of note, both SRI and BICLA are
complex composite instruments that capture similar relevant clinical concepts and domains of
the disease and are both considered clinically meaningful endpoints for evaluating a treatment
effect on clinical response/reduction in disease activity in SLE. 47 ,48 Given the complex clinical
development and changes to the primary endpoint for one of the phase 3 studies, the review
team carefully considered the data supporting substantial evidence of effectiveness. I agree
with the team’s assessment and conclusions that the substantial evidence of effectiveness is
provided by study 04 with confirmatory evidence from the other two studies (1013 and 05) and
supportive mechanistic rationale. Study 04 which represents an adequate and well-controlled
trial showing favorable and statistically significant changes on the pre-specified, clinically
meaningful primary and secondary endpoints when comparing anifrolumab 300 mg IV versus
placebo. Because the phase 2 trial (1013) was designed only as a proof of concept and doseranging study with inadequate multiplicity control and study 05, the first phase 3 trial, failed on
its primary endpoint, these cannot be considered additional successful adequate and wellcontrolled trials However, the review team concluded, and I agree, that when these trials are
considered in combination, the consistent results observed in studies 1013 and 05 do provide
confirmatory evidence that support the effectiveness of anifrolumab 300 mg IV in adults with
moderate to severe SLE, a disease with high unmet need.

47

Guidance for Industry: Systemic Lupus Erythematosus—Developing Medical Products for Treatment (June 2010);
https://www.fda.gov/media/71150/download
48
Salmon J, Niewold T, A Successful Trial for Lupus - How Good Is Good Enough?, N Engl J Med. 2020 Jan
16;382(3):287-288

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE

Across the three studies, there are three randomized comparisons, each with adequate
duration (52 weeks), that support the claim that anifrolumab 300 mg IV yields higher BICLA
response rates compared to placebo. Further, two of the three studies provide support that
anifrolumab 300 mg IV yields higher SRI-4 response rates compared to placebo at week 52. The
review team also provided a plausible explanation of the discordant SRI-4 and BICLA results in
study 05, as detailed in the document. In all three studies, additional confirmatory evidence
was noted in the analyses of the secondary endpoints, including a greater proportion of
subjects on anifrolumab than placebo able to reduce oral corticosteroid use and a lower
proportion of subjects on anifrolumab experiencing a flare of SLE disease activity, further
supporting the effectiveness of anifrolumab. For transparency, these results, including the
results from the failed study 05 should be described and appropriately annotated in the
product labeling.
The primary safety assessment of anifrolumab was informed by a blinded controlled safety
comparison from the three studies (1013, 05, and 04) in 459 subjects treated with anifrolumab
300 IV dosing regimen compared with 466 on placebo for 52 weeks. Controlled long-term
safety assessments beyond 52 weeks were also submitted for patients who rolled over from the
phase 3 studies 04 and 05, even though this assessment is still ongoing. Overall, the safety
profile of anifrolumab 300 mg IV every 4 weeks appeared similar to placebo, with the exception
of certain infections (including herpes zoster) and infusion related reactions (including two
cases of angioedema). In that long-term safety comparisons dataset, herpes zoster and other
viral infections continued to occur at a higher rate in patients treated with anifrolumab 300 mg,
and fatal respiratory infections occurred only in the anifrolumab 300 mg arm, including
pneumonia and COVID-19. Malignancy was an adverse event of special interest because of the
potential in rodent models of IFNAR1 blockade, increased carcinogenic potential has been
observed. In the anifrolumab controlled safety database malignancies, excluding nonmelanoma skin cancer, were balanced. Safety information was also assessed for 150 mg and
1000 mg dosing regimens used in studies 05 and 1013, respectively. One case of anaphylaxis
was reported in a subject receiving the 150 mg dose, warranting a contraindication in patients
with a history of anaphylaxis with anifrolumab. The potential safety risks can be adequately
managed via appropriate product labeling.
In summary, the overall benefit risk profile is favorable, and the Division recommended action
for this application is Approval, with appropriate labeling. This recommendation also considers
the discussion and input from the FDA Medical Policy and Program Review Council on January
22, 2021.
Post-marketing required studies will include a pregnancy registry, additional pregnancy study, a
lactation study, and a PREA study in pediatric patients with SLE 5 years of age and older. The
OPQ review team also recommended a post-marketing commitment for the implementation of
(b) (4)
anifrolumab drug product.

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 4834322

BLA 761123 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Saphnelo (anifrolumab-fnia) for adults with SLE
If approved, anifrolumab, a first-in-class biologic with a unique mechanism of action, will
provide a new treatment option for patients with SLE.

15. Office Director (Signatory authority) Comments
I concur with the recommendation of the Division of Rheumatology and Transplant Medicine to
approve anifrolumab-fnia for the treatment of adults with moderate to severe systemic lupus
erythematosus (SLE) who are receiving standard therapy. Anifrolumab is a first-in-class type I
interferon (IFN) receptor antagonist and has been shown to neutralize the type I IFN gene
signature in patients with a high baseline gene signature. Approval of anifrolumab will provide
another therapeutic option for patients with this serious and life-threatening disease. The
recommended dose is 300 mg, administered IV every 4 weeks.
The efficacy of anifrolumab was evaluated in three 52-week treatment period, multicenter,
randomized placebo-controlled trials. Taken together, these studies demonstrate that
anifrolumab confers clinically meaningful benefit relative to placebo as assessed by the
composite endpoints of SRI-4 and BICLA. Efficacy was maintained throughout the 52 week
treatment period. Additionally, evidence for reduction in the use of oral corticosteroids in all
three trials, and in the reduction of SLE flares in one trial was also provided. The efficacy of
anifrolumab has not been evaluated in patients with severe active lupus nephritis or in severe
active central nervous system lupus.
The safety of anifrolumab was assessed in the three trials over the 52-week treatment period
and compared to placebo. Overall, the safety profile of anifrolumab was similar to that of
placebo, with the exception of certain infections, notably herpes zoster, and infusion-related
reactions. Product labeling will adequately convey the drug’s risks.
Postmarketing studies will be required to assess the safety and pharmacokinetics of
anifrolumab when used with standard therapy in pediatric SLE patients ages 5 to 17 years, to
assess adverse pregnancy outcomes and adverse effects on exposed infants, and to measure
the presence of the drug in human breast milk.

Population Pharmacokinetics
The population PK model was supported by 6049 anifrolumab concentrations from 670 subjects
(664 SLE patients and 6 healthy volunteers) from studies 1013, 02, 04, 05 and the IV arm of 06.
16.4.2.1. Structural Model
The pharmacokinetics (PK) of anifrolumab was described by a 2-compartment model with
parallel first-order elimination and target mediated drug disposition with Quasi-steady-state
(QSS) approximation (Dua et al 2015), where the binding rate of anifrolumab to IFN- R1 is
balanced by the sum of the dissociation and internalization of anifrolumab-IFN- R1 complex. In
QSS approximation, kon · Ab · R (kint + koff) · [Ab·C] = 0; and kss = (kint + koff) / kon.
Figure 20. Anifrolumab PK Model

Ab, anifrolumab concentration in the central compartment; Abp, anifrolumab concentration in the peripheral tissue
compartment; Ab·R, anifrolumab–IFN- R1 complex concentration; CLRES, clearance by the reticuloendothelial system but is
simplified as CL hereafter; IFN- R1, type I IFN receptor subunit 1; kint, internalization rate constant;koff, dissociation rate
constant; kon, association rate constant; ksyn, kdeg, endogenous production and degradation rate constant of IFN- R1; PK,
pharmacokinetic; Q, intercompartmental clearance; R, free receptor concentration; R0, free receptor concentration at baseline.
(source: Figure 1 in Population PK Report)

16.4.2.2. Covariate Effect
Demographics, laboratory test results, disease characteristics, SLE-related treatment at baseline
were evaluated for their effect on anifrolumab PK, these baseline patients characteristics are
summarized in Table 75 to Table 78. Due to the very low prevalence of ADA (6.9% [46/670]),
the effect of ADA on anifrolumab CL was not evaluated. A post-hoc analysis was performed;
there was no evidence that ADA positive or persistent ADA positive impacted PK.

Body weight was identified to be the most statistically significant covariate on anifrolumab
clearance (CL), followed by type I IFN gene signature test status on CL; IFN gene signature status
was found to have a larger drop in objective functional value on linear CL than R0. In addition to
IFN gene signature status on CL, baseline body weight on CL was also found to be a statistically
significant covariate. Body weight was also found to be a statistically significant covariate on
central volume of distribution (Vc).
To account for the increasing trend in concentrations over time, an empirical sigmoidal time
dependent function was added to the linear CL of the final model:

The final PopPK model parameter estimates are given in Table 79.

(source: Table 15 in Population PK Report)

Age, sex, region, ethnicity, race, eGFR, CLcr, AST, ALT, total bilirubin, albumin, ADA status, and

mofetil, steroids, Mizorbine, NSAIDs, ACE inhibitors, HMG-CoA reductase inhibitors) were not
found to be significant on CL. Patients with high UPCR appeared to have higher CL, but the
impact was not significant. The exclusion criteria for UPCR was 2 mg/mg in the SLE clinical trials.

16.4.2.3. Conclusion
The Applicant’s population pharmacokinetic analysis is replicable and acceptable. No covariate
had clinically meaningful impact on the PK characteristics of anifrolumab based on population
PK analysis.
Exposure Response Relationship
The exposure response (ER) analysis for BICLA, SRI(4), annualized flare rate, and herpes zoster
incidence rate in the two Phase 3 studies (04 and 05) using ITT population, completers, and
completers with high baseline type 1 IFN gene signature were used to support the dose
selection along with the Phase 2 dose ranging study 1013.
To evaluate the exposure-response relationship of anifrolumab for efficacy, the pooled phase
3 (studies 04 and 05) population was analyzed using the full exposure-response analysis set. All
patients in the full exposure-response analysis set are also referred to as ‘all-comers’ (or intentto-treat, ITT) in subsequent sections. Patients who discontinued from treatment were
considered non-responders for BICLA, SRI(4), OCS reduction, and CLASI. Thus, in order to
examine the exposure-response relationship in the absence of confounding effect with
discontinuation, additional analyses of BICLA and SRI(4) were performed in the subgroup of
patients who completed treatment for 1) all-comers, and 2) patients who were type I IFN gene
signature test high.
Based on the treatment received and anifrolumab exposure quartiles (Q1 to Q4), patients were
divided into 6 groups: placebo, 150 mg, 300 mg Q1, 300 mg Q2, 300 mg Q3, and 300 mg Q4.
The reviewer’s assessment of exposure response relationship of different efficacy endpoints
(BICLA, SRI(4), OCS reduction, annualized flare rate) and safety events (herpes zoster) were
based on the divides mentioned above in different patient subgroups (all-comer/ITT,
completers, completers who were type I IFN gen signature test high).

Baseline demographics and disease characteristics for each patient population mentioned
above are summarized in Table 80 to Table 82 below with the corresponding anifrolumab
exposure (average concentration, Cave).

The demographics and baseline disease characteristics were similar across different patient
population subgroups in the corresponding treatment group and exposure quartiles, indicating
the dropouts were not demographics and baseline disease characteristics dependent. However,
patients with lower exposure in 300 mg treatment group tend to have a higher bodyweight at
baseline, as bodyweight is positively correlated with anifrolumab clearance. Also, patients with
lower exposure in 300 mg treatment group tend to have a higher type I IFN gene signature,
SLEDAI, and oral corticosteroids dose at baseline. Due to these unbalanced disease
characteristics at baseline, the positive exposure response relationship with in 300 mg
treatment group (as shown in grey color in all the following analysis) unnecessarily indicated a
positive exposure response relationship below or beyond the observed exposure range in 300
mg Q4W dosing regimen.
16.4.3.2. BICLA
BICLA in High IFN Completer
The Applicant conducted a logistic regression analysis of the BICLA response rate by evaluating
the significance of a continuous Cave in type I IFN test high patients who completed treatment
(150 mg and 300 mg). The analysis results are depicted in Figure 32, and the parameter
estimates are listed in Table 83.

Figure 32. Predicted BICLA response rate at week 52 in patients with type I IFN gene signature
test high who completed treatment (full exposure response analysis set)

(b) (4)

The carcinogenic and genotoxic potential of anifrolumab‑fnia
have not been
evaluated. In rodent models of IFNAR1 blockade, increased carcinogenic potential has
been observed. The clinical relevance of these findings is unknown.
Effects on male and female fertility have not been directly evaluated in animal studies.
No anifrolumab-fnia-related adverse effects on indirect measures of male or female
fertility, based on semen analysis, spermatogenesis staging, menses cycle, organ
weights and histopathological findings in the reproductive organs were observed in 9month repeat-dose toxicity studies in cynomolgus monkeys at doses up to 50 mg/kg

11

Integrated Summary and Safety Evaluation

Presented above is the Applicant’s proposed draft labeling text for the SAPHNELO
(anifrolumab-fnia) prescribing information dated July 31, 2020 (SDN 1) and the
nonclinical revisions to the Applicant’s proposed labeling. The underlined text is
recommended for insertion and the strikethrough text is recommended for deletion from
the Applicant’s proposed text. Below are rationales for the proposed changes for each
section.
INDICATIONS AND USAGE
SAPHNELO is a human IgG1κ that binds to IFNAR1. Since this is a first-in-class
product targeting IFNAR, the Applicant proposed “type I interferon (IFN) receptor(b) (4)
” as the established pharmacological class (EPC) for anifrolumab. The
relevant pharmacology data as listed below were reviewed by Dr. Luqi Pei on
01/18/2011 under IND 101849.
 Anifrolumab has high affinity for IFNAR1 expressed on human and cynomolgus
monkey peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) with KD of 0.29 and 0.65 nM,
respectively (study# RIA546-0002).
 Anifrolumab bound to the sub-domain 3 of human IFNAR1 (265-307) with residues
276-279 identified as the critical epitope (study# RIA546-0004).
 Anifrolumab competitively inhibited the binding of IFN-α-2a to IFNAR in Daudi cells
(study# RIA546-0003).
 Anifrolumab inhibited
 IFN-stimulated response elements (ISRE) activation induced by all the
recombinant human IFN-α subtypes (study# RIA546-0006) and SLE sera (study#
RIA546-0008),
 expression of Type-I IFN responsive genes (USP18, HERC5/6, IFIT1/3/5,
G1P2/3, MX1/2, OAS1/2, IFI44, IFI44L, Ly6E) from PBMCs by sera of SLE
patients (study# RIA546-0005),
 expression of differentiation markers (CD123 and CD38) and cytokines/
chemokines (IL-6, IL-10, MCP-1, MCP-2) from monocytes by leukocyte-IFNs
(study# RIA546-0005).
These data suggest that anifrolumab is specifically targeting IFNAR1 and inhibiting type
I IFN signaling, thereby blocking the biologic activity of type I IFNs. Our internal EPC

6

BLA # 761123

Reviewer: Xiaochun Chen, PhD

to be around 58 (= 21600 x 4 / 1472). A factor of 4 is applied to match the duration of
AUC values before the comparison, i.e., 7 days vs 28 days.
